# Textos de Tomás

## Prima pars (I)

* [ST I, q.1, a.1 — Whether, besides philosophy, any further doctrine is required](st-i-q1-a1.md) - Aquinas argues that a revealed doctrine is necessary because man is directed to God as to an end that surpasses the grasp of reason.
* [ST I, q.1, a.2 — Whether sacred doctrine is a science](st-i-q1-a2.md) - Sacred doctrine is a science.
* [ST I, q.1, a.3 — Whether sacred doctrine is one science](st-i-q1-a3.md) - Sacred doctrine is one science.
* [ST I, q.1, a.4 — Whether sacred doctrine is a practical science](st-i-q1-a4.md) - Sacred doctrine, being one, extends to things which belong to different philosophical sciences because it considers in each the same formal aspect, namely, so far as they can be known through…
* [ST I, q.1, a.5 — Whether sacred doctrine is nobler than other sciences](st-i-q1-a5.md) - Since this science is partly speculative and partly practical, it transcends all others speculative and practical.
* [ST I, q.1, a.6 — Whether this doctrine is the same as wisdom](st-i-q1-a6.md) - This doctrine is wisdom above all human wisdom;
* [ST I, q.1, a.7 — Whether God is the object of this science](st-i-q1-a7.md) - God is the object of this science.
* [ST I, q.1, a.8 — Whether sacred doctrine is a matter of argument](st-i-q1-a8.md) - Sacred doctrine argues from the articles of faith as its principles and can answer objections against them.
* [ST I, q.1, a.9 — Whether Holy Scripture should use metaphors](st-i-q1-a9.md) - It is befitting Holy Writ to put forward divine and spiritual truths by means of comparisons with material things.
* [ST I, q.1, a.10 — Whether in Holy Scripture a word may have several senses](st-i-q1-a10.md) - The author of Holy Writ is God, in whose power it is to signify His meaning, not by words only (as man also can do), but also by things themselves.
* [ST I, q.2, a.1 — Whether the existence of God is self-evident](st-i-q2-a1.md) - A proposition can be self-evident in itself but not to us.
* [ST I, q.2, a.2 — Whether it can be demonstrated that God exists](st-i-q2-a2.md) - There are two kinds of demonstration: from the cause (propter quid) and from the effect (quia).
* [ST I, q.2, a.3 — Whether God exists (the five ways)](st-i-q2-a3.md) - The five ways proceed from motion, efficient causality, contingency, gradation of perfection, and the governance of things.
* [ST I, q.3, a.1 — Whether God is a body](st-i-q3-a1.md) - It is absolutely true that God is not a body;
* [ST I, q.3, a.2 — Whether God is composed of matter and form](st-i-q3-a2.md) - It is impossible that matter should exist in God.
* [ST I, q.3, a.3 — Whether God is the same as His essence or nature](st-i-q3-a3.md) - God is the same as His essence or nature.
* [ST I, q.3, a.4 — Whether essence and existence are the same in God](st-i-q3-a4.md) - In God essence and existence (esse) are identical: God does not have being, he is his own act of being.
* [ST I, q.3, a.5 — Whether God is contained in a genus](st-i-q3-a5.md) - A thing can be in a genus in two ways;
* [ST I, q.3, a.6 — Whether in God there are any accidents](st-i-q3-a6.md) - From all we have said, it is clear there can be no accident in God.
* [ST I, q.3, a.7 — Whether God is altogether simple](st-i-q3-a7.md) - God is absolutely simple: he is not composed of parts, of matter and form, of essence and existence, of genus and difference, or of substance and accidents.
* [ST I, q.3, a.8 — Whether God enters into the composition of other things](st-i-q3-a8.md) - On this point there have been three errors.
* [ST I, q.4, a.1 — Whether God is perfect](st-i-q4-a1.md) - As the Philosopher relates (Metaph.
* [ST I, q.4, a.2 — Whether the perfections of all things are in God](st-i-q4-a2.md) - All created perfections are in God.
* [ST I, q.4, a.3 — Whether any creature can be like God](st-i-q4-a3.md) - Since likeness is based upon agreement or communication in form, it varies according to the many modes of communication in form.
* [ST I, q.5, a.1 — Whether goodness differs really from being](st-i-q5-a1.md) - Goodness and being are the same in reality and differ only in idea.
* [ST I, q.5, a.2 — Whether goodness is prior in idea to being](st-i-q5-a2.md) - In idea being is prior to goodness.
* [ST I, q.5, a.3 — Whether every being is good](st-i-q5-a3.md) - Every being, as being, is good.
* [ST I, q.5, a.4 — Whether goodness has the aspect of a final cause](st-i-q5-a4.md) - Since goodness is that which all things desire, and since this has the aspect of an end, it is clear that goodness implies the aspect of an end.
* [ST I, q.5, a.5 — Whether the essence of goodness consists in mode, species and order](st-i-q5-a5.md) - Everything is said to be good so far as it is perfect;
* [ST I, q.5, a.6 — Whether goodness is rightly divided into the virtuous, the useful and the pleasant](st-i-q5-a6.md) - This division properly concerns human goodness.
* [ST I, q.6, a.1 — Whether God is good](st-i-q6-a1.md) - To be good belongs pre-eminently to God.
* [ST I, q.6, a.2 — Whether God is the supreme good](st-i-q6-a2.md) - God is the supreme good simply, and not only as existing in any genus or order of things.
* [ST I, q.6, a.3 — Whether to be essentially good belongs to God alone](st-i-q6-a3.md) - God alone is good essentially.
* [ST I, q.6, a.4 — Whether all things are good by the divine goodness](st-i-q6-a4.md) - As regards relative things, we must admit extrinsic denomination;
* [ST I, q.7, a.1 — Whether God is infinite](st-i-q7-a1.md) - All the ancient philosophers attribute infinitude to the first principle, as is said (Phys.
* [ST I, q.7, a.2 — Whether anything but God can be essentially infinite](st-i-q7-a2.md) - Things other than God can be relatively infinite, but not absolutely infinite.
* [ST I, q.7, a.3 — Whether an actually infinite magnitude can exist](st-i-q7-a3.md) - It is one thing to be infinite in essence, and another to be infinite in magnitude.
* [ST I, q.7, a.4 — Whether an infinite multitude can exist](st-i-q7-a4.md) - A twofold opinion exists on this subject.
* [ST I, q.8, a.1 — Whether God is in all things](st-i-q8-a1.md) - God is in all things;
* [ST I, q.8, a.2 — Whether God is everywhere](st-i-q8-a2.md) - Since place is a thing, to be in place can be understood in a twofold sense;
* [ST I, q.8, a.3 — Whether God is everywhere by essence, presence and power](st-i-q8-a3.md) - God is said to be in a thing in two ways;
* [ST I, q.8, a.4 — Whether to be everywhere belongs to God alone](st-i-q8-a4.md) - To be everywhere primarily and absolutely, is proper to God.
* [ST I, q.9, a.1 — Whether God is altogether immutable](st-i-q9-a1.md) - From what precedes, it is shown that God is altogether immutable.
* [ST I, q.9, a.2 — Whether to be immutable belongs to God alone](st-i-q9-a2.md) - God alone is altogether immutable;
* [ST I, q.10, a.1 — Whether this is a good definition of eternity, "The simultaneously-whole and perfect possession of interminable life"](st-i-q10-a1.md) - As we attain to the knowledge of simple things by way of compound things, so must we reach to the knowledge of eternity by means of time, which is nothing but the numbering of movement by "before"…
* [ST I, q.10, a.2 — Whether God is eternal](st-i-q10-a2.md) - The idea of eternity follows immutability, as the idea of time follows movement, as appears from the preceding article.
* [ST I, q.10, a.3 — Whether to be eternal belongs to God alone](st-i-q10-a3.md) - Eternity truly and properly so called is in God alone, because eternity follows on immutability;
* [ST I, q.10, a.4 — Whether eternity differs from time](st-i-q10-a4.md) - It is manifest that time and eternity are not the same.
* [ST I, q.10, a.5 — The difference of aeviternity and time](st-i-q10-a5.md) - Aeviternity differs from time, and from eternity, as the mean between them both.
* [ST I, q.10, a.6 — Whether there is only one aeviternity](st-i-q10-a6.md) - A twofold opinion exists on this subject.
* [ST I, q.11, a.1 — Whether "one" adds anything to "being"](st-i-q11-a1.md) - "One" does not add any reality to "being";
* [ST I, q.11, a.2 — Whether "one" and "many" are opposed to each other](st-i-q11-a2.md) - "One" is opposed to "many," but in various ways.
* [ST I, q.11, a.3 — Whether God is one](st-i-q11-a3.md) - It can be shown from these three sources that God is one.
* [ST I, q.11, a.4 — Whether God is supremely one](st-i-q11-a4.md) - Since "one" is an undivided being, if anything is supremely "one" it must be supremely being, and supremely undivided.
* [ST I, q.12, a.1 — Whether any created intellect can see the essence of God](st-i-q12-a1.md) - The created intellect can see God's essence, for otherwise the natural desire to know the cause of what we see would be in vain and our ultimate happiness would be impossible.
* [ST I, q.12, a.2 — Whether the essence of God is seen by the created intellect through an image](st-i-q12-a2.md) - Two things are required both for sensible and for intellectual vision—viz.
* [ST I, q.12, a.3 — Whether the essence of God can be seen with the bodily eye](st-i-q12-a3.md) - It is impossible for God to be seen by the sense of sight, or by any other sense, or faculty of the sensitive power.
* [ST I, q.12, a.4 — Whether any created intellect by its natural powers can see the Divine essence](st-i-q12-a4.md) - It is impossible for any created intellect to see the essence of God by its own natural power.
* [ST I, q.12, a.5 — Whether the created intellect needs any created light in order to see the essence of God](st-i-q12-a5.md) - Everything which is raised up to what exceeds its nature, must be prepared by some disposition above its nature;
* [ST I, q.12, a.6 — Whether of those who see the essence of God, one sees more perfectly than another](st-i-q12-a6.md) - Of those who see the essence of God, one sees Him more perfectly than another.
* [ST I, q.12, a.7 — Whether those who see the essence of God comprehend Him](st-i-q12-a7.md) - It is impossible for any created intellect to comprehend God;
* [ST I, q.12, a.8 — Whether those who see the essence of God see all in God](st-i-q12-a8.md) - The created intellect, in seeing the divine essence, does not see in it all that God does or can do.
* [ST I, q.12, a.9 — Whether what is seen in God by those who see the Divine essence, is seen through any similitude](st-i-q12-a9.md) - Those who see the divine essence see what they see in God not by any likeness, but by the divine essence itself united to their intellect.
* [ST I, q.12, a.10 — Whether those who see the essence of God see all they see in it at the same time](st-i-q12-a10.md) - What is seen in the Word is seen not successively, but at the same time.
* [ST I, q.12, a.11 — Whether anyone in this life can see the essence of God](st-i-q12-a11.md) - God cannot be seen in His essence by a mere human being, except he be separated from this mortal life.
* [ST I, q.12, a.12 — Whether God can be known in this life by natural reason](st-i-q12-a12.md) - Our natural knowledge begins from the senses, so reason can know of God only what can be known from sensible effects: that he exists, that he is the first cause of all things, and that he differs from all things he…
* [ST I, q.12, a.13 — Whether by grace a higher knowledge of God can be obtained than by natural reason](st-i-q12-a13.md) - We have a more perfect knowledge of God by grace than by natural reason.
* [ST I, q.13, a.1 — Whether a name can be given to God](st-i-q13-a1.md) - Since according to the Philosopher (Peri Herm.
* [ST I, q.13, a.2 — Whether any name can be applied to God substantially](st-i-q13-a2.md) - Negative names applied to God, or signifying His relation to creatures manifestly do not at all signify His substance, but rather express the distance of the creature from Him, or His relation to…
* [ST I, q.13, a.3 — Whether any name can be applied to God in its literal sense](st-i-q13-a3.md) - According to the preceding article, our knowledge of God is derived from the perfections which flow from Him to creatures, which perfections are in God in a more eminent way than in creatures.
* [ST I, q.13, a.4 — Whether names applied to God are synonymous](st-i-q13-a4.md) - These names spoken of God are not synonymous.
* [ST I, q.13, a.5 — Whether what is said of God and of creatures is univocally predicated of them](st-i-q13-a5.md) - Names such as 'good' or 'wise' are said of God and creatures neither univocally nor purely equivocally, but analogically, according to proportion: the perfection exists in God primarily and in creatures by participation.
* [ST I, q.13, a.6 — Whether names predicated of God are predicated primarily of creatures](st-i-q13-a6.md) - In names predicated of many in an analogical sense, all are predicated because they have reference to some one thing;
* [ST I, q.13, a.7 — Whether names which imply relation to creatures are predicated of God temporally](st-i-q13-a7.md) - The names which import relation to creatures are applied to God temporally, and not from eternity.
* [ST I, q.13, a.8 — Whether this name "God" is a name of the nature](st-i-q13-a8.md) - Whence a name is imposed, and what the name signifies are not always the same thing.
* [ST I, q.13, a.9 — Whether this name "God" is communicable](st-i-q13-a9.md) - A name is communicable in two ways: properly, and by similitude.
* [ST I, q.13, a.10 — Whether this name "God" is applied to God univocally by nature, by participation, and according to opinion](st-i-q13-a10.md) - This name "God" in the three aforesaid significations is taken neither univocally nor equivocally, but analogically.
* [ST I, q.13, a.11 — Whether this name, HE WHO IS, is the most proper name of God](st-i-q13-a11.md) - This name HE WHO IS is most properly applied to God, for three reasons: First, because of its signification.
* [ST I, q.13, a.12 — Whether affirmative propositions can be formed about God](st-i-q13-a12.md) - True affirmative propositions can be formed about God.
* [ST I, q.14, a.1 — Whether there is knowledge in God](st-i-q14-a1.md) - Knowing beings differ from non-knowing beings in that they can possess the form of another thing as well as their own.
* [ST I, q.14, a.2 — Whether God understands Himself](st-i-q14-a2.md) - God understands Himself through Himself.
* [ST I, q.14, a.3 — Whether God comprehends Himself](st-i-q14-a3.md) - God perfectly comprehends Himself, as can be thus proved.
* [ST I, q.14, a.4 — Whether the act of God's intellect is His substance](st-i-q14-a4.md) - It must be said that the act of God's intellect is His substance.
* [ST I, q.14, a.5 — Whether God knows things other than Himself](st-i-q14-a5.md) - God necessarily knows things other than Himself.
* [ST I, q.14, a.6 — Whether God knows things other than Himself by proper knowledge](st-i-q14-a6.md) - Some have erred on this point, saying that God knows things other than Himself only in general, that is, only as beings.
* [ST I, q.14, a.7 — Whether the knowledge of God is discursive](st-i-q14-a7.md) - In the divine knowledge there is no discursion;
* [ST I, q.14, a.8 — Whether the knowledge of God is the cause of things](st-i-q14-a8.md) - The knowledge of God is the cause of things.
* [ST I, q.14, a.9 — Whether God has knowledge of things that are not](st-i-q14-a9.md) - God knows all things whatsoever that in any way are.
* [ST I, q.14, a.10 — Whether God knows evil things](st-i-q14-a10.md) - Whoever knows a thing perfectly, must know all that can be accidental to it.
* [ST I, q.14, a.11 — Whether God knows singular things](st-i-q14-a11.md) - God knows singular things.
* [ST I, q.14, a.12 — Whether God can know infinite things](st-i-q14-a12.md) - Since God knows not only things actual but also things possible to Himself or to created things, as shown above (Article 9), and as these must be infinite, it must be held that He knows infinite…
* [ST I, q.14, a.13 — Whether the knowledge of God is of future contingent things](st-i-q14-a13.md) - Since as was shown above (Article 9), God knows all things;
* [ST I, q.14, a.14 — Whether God knows enunciable things](st-i-q14-a14.md) - Since it is in the power of our intellect to form enunciations, and since God knows whatever is in His own power or in that of creatures, as said above (Article 9), it follows of necessity that…
* [ST I, q.14, a.15 — Whether the knowledge of God is variable](st-i-q14-a15.md) - Since the knowledge of God is His substance, as is clear from the foregoing (Article 4), just as His substance is altogether immutable, as shown above (I:9:1), so His knowledge likewise must be…
* [ST I, q.14, a.16 — Whether God has a speculative knowledge of things](st-i-q14-a16.md) - Some knowledge is speculative only;
* [ST I, q.15, a.1 — Whether there are ideas](st-i-q15-a1.md) - It is necessary to suppose ideas in the divine mind.
* [ST I, q.15, a.2 — Whether ideas are many](st-i-q15-a2.md) - It must necessarily be held that ideas are many.
* [ST I, q.15, a.3 — Whether there are ideas of all things that God knows](st-i-q15-a3.md) - As ideas, according to Plato, are principles of the knowledge of things and of their generation, an idea has this twofold office, as it exists in the mind of God.
* [ST I, q.16, a.1 — Whether truth resides only in the intellect](st-i-q16-a1.md) - Truth is found primarily in the intellect and secondarily in things, insofar as they are related to an intellect.
* [ST I, q.16, a.2 — Whether truth resides only in the intellect composing and dividing](st-i-q16-a2.md) - As stated before, truth resides, in its primary aspect, in the intellect.
* [ST I, q.16, a.3 — Whether the true and being are convertible terms](st-i-q16-a3.md) - As good has the nature of what is desirable, so truth is related to knowledge.
* [ST I, q.16, a.4 — Whether good is logically prior to the true](st-i-q16-a4.md) - Although the good and the true are convertible with being, as to suppositum, yet they differ logically.
* [ST I, q.16, a.5 — Whether God is truth](st-i-q16-a5.md) - As said above (Article 1), truth is found in the intellect according as it apprehends a thing as it is;
* [ST I, q.16, a.6 — Whether there is only one truth, according to which all things are true](st-i-q16-a6.md) - In one sense truth, whereby all things are true, is one, and in another sense it is not.
* [ST I, q.16, a.7 — Whether created truth is eternal](st-i-q16-a7.md) - The truth of enunciations is no other than the truth of the intellect.
* [ST I, q.16, a.8 — Whether truth is immutable](st-i-q16-a8.md) - Truth, properly speaking, resides only in the intellect, as said before (Article 1);
* [ST I, q.17, a.1 — Whether falsity exists in things](st-i-q17-a1.md) - Since true and false are opposed, and since opposites stand in relation to the same thing, we must needs seek falsity, where primarily we find truth;
* [ST I, q.17, a.2 — Whether there is falsity in the senses](st-i-q17-a2.md) - Falsity is not to be sought in the senses except as truth is in them.
* [ST I, q.17, a.3 — Whether falsity is in the intellect](st-i-q17-a3.md) - Just as a thing has being by its proper form, so the knowing faculty has knowledge by the likeness of the thing known.
* [ST I, q.17, a.4 — Whether true and false are contraries](st-i-q17-a4.md) - True and false are opposed as contraries, and not, as some have said, as affirmation and negation.
* [ST I, q.18, a.1 — Whether to live belongs to all natural things](st-i-q18-a1.md) - We can gather to what things life belongs, and to what it does not, from such things as manifestly possess life.
* [ST I, q.18, a.2 — Whether life is an operation](st-i-q18-a2.md) - As is clear from what has been said (I:17:3), our intellect, which takes cognizance of the essence of a thing as its proper object, gains knowledge from sense, of which the proper objects are…
* [ST I, q.18, a.3 — Whether life is properly attributed to God](st-i-q18-a3.md) - Life is in the highest degree properly in God.
* [ST I, q.18, a.4 — Whether all things are life in God](st-i-q18-a4.md) - In God to live is to understand, as before stated (Article 3).
* [ST I, q.19, a.1 — Whether there is will in God](st-i-q19-a1.md) - There is will in God, as there is intellect: since will follows upon intellect.
* [ST I, q.19, a.2 — Whether God wills things apart from Himself](st-i-q19-a2.md) - God wills not only Himself, but other things apart from Himself.
* [ST I, q.19, a.3 — Whether whatever God wills He wills necessarily](st-i-q19-a3.md) - There are two ways in which a thing is said to be necessary, namely, absolutely, and by supposition.
* [ST I, q.19, a.4 — Whether the will of God is the cause of things](st-i-q19-a4.md) - We must hold that the will of God is the cause of things;
* [ST I, q.19, a.5 — Whether any cause can be assigned to the divine will](st-i-q19-a5.md) - In no wise has the will of God a cause.
* [ST I, q.19, a.6 — Whether the will of God is always fulfilled](st-i-q19-a6.md) - The will of God must needs always be fulfilled.
* [ST I, q.19, a.7 — Whether the will of God is changeable](st-i-q19-a7.md) - The will of God is entirely unchangeable.
* [ST I, q.19, a.8 — Whether the will of God imposes necessity on the things willed](st-i-q19-a8.md) - Because the divine will is supremely efficacious, not only do the things God wills come about, but they come about in the way he wills: some necessarily and some contingently.
* [ST I, q.19, a.9 — Whether God wills evils](st-i-q19-a9.md) - Since the ratio of good is the ratio of appetibility, as said before (I:5:1), and since evil is opposed to good, it is impossible that any evil, as such, should be sought for by the appetite,…
* [ST I, q.19, a.10 — Whether God has free-will](st-i-q19-a10.md) - We have free-will with respect to what we will not of necessity, nor be natural instinct.
* [ST I, q.19, a.11 — Whether the will of expression is to be distinguished in God](st-i-q19-a11.md) - Some things are said of God in their strict sense;
* [ST I, q.19, a.12 — Whether five expressions of will are rightly assigned to the divine will](st-i-q19-a12.md) - By these signs we name the expression of will by which we are accustomed to show that we will something.
* [ST I, q.20, a.1 — Whether love exists in God](st-i-q20-a1.md) - We must needs assert that in God there is love: because love is the first movement of the will and of every appetitive faculty.
* [ST I, q.20, a.2 — Whether God loves all things](st-i-q20-a2.md) - God loves all existing things, since to love is to will good to someone and God's will is the cause of all goodness in things.
* [ST I, q.20, a.3 — Whether God loves all things equally](st-i-q20-a3.md) - Since to love a thing is to will it good, in a twofold way anything may be loved more, or less.
* [ST I, q.20, a.4 — Whether God always loves more the better things](st-i-q20-a4.md) - It must needs be, according to what has been said before, that God loves more the better things.
* [ST I, q.21, a.1 — Whether there is justice in God](st-i-q21-a1.md) - There are two kinds of justice.
* [ST I, q.21, a.2 — Whether the justice of God is truth](st-i-q21-a2.md) - Truth consists in the equation of mind and thing, as said above (I:16:1).
* [ST I, q.21, a.3 — Whether mercy can be attributed to God](st-i-q21-a3.md) - Mercy is especially to be attributed to God, as seen in its effect, but not as an affection of passion.
* [ST I, q.21, a.4 — Whether in every work of God there are mercy and justice](st-i-q21-a4.md) - Mercy and truth are necessarily found in all God's works, if mercy be taken to mean the removal of any kind of defect.
* [ST I, q.22, a.1 — Whether providence can suitably be attributed to God](st-i-q22-a1.md) - It is necessary to attribute providence to God.
* [ST I, q.22, a.2 — Whether everything is subject to the providence of God](st-i-q22-a2.md) - All things, even individual and contingent events, are subject to divine providence, because God's causality extends to everything that has being.
* [ST I, q.22, a.3 — Whether God has immediate providence over everything](st-i-q22-a3.md) - Two things belong to providence —namely, the type of the order of things foreordained towards an end;
* [ST I, q.22, a.4 — Whether providence imposes any necessity on things foreseen](st-i-q22-a4.md) - Divine providence imposes necessity upon some things;
* [ST I, q.23, a.1 — Whether men are predestined by God](st-i-q23-a1.md) - It is fitting that God should predestine men.
* [ST I, q.23, a.2 — Whether predestination places anything in the predestined](st-i-q23-a2.md) - Predestination is not anything in the predestined;
* [ST I, q.23, a.3 — Whether God reprobates any man](st-i-q23-a3.md) - God does reprobate some.
* [ST I, q.23, a.4 — Whether the predestined are chosen by God](st-i-q23-a4.md) - Predestination presupposes election in the order of reason;
* [ST I, q.23, a.5 — Whether the foreknowledge of merits is the cause of predestination](st-i-q23-a5.md) - Since predestination includes will, as was said above (Article 4), the reason of predestination must be sought for in the same way as was the reason of the will of God.
* [ST I, q.23, a.6 — Whether predestination is certain](st-i-q23-a6.md) - Predestination most certainly and infallibly takes effect;
* [ST I, q.23, a.7 — Whether the number of the predestined is certain](st-i-q23-a7.md) - The number of the predestined is certain.
* [ST I, q.23, a.8 — Whether predestination can be furthered by the prayers of the saints](st-i-q23-a8.md) - Concerning this question, there were different errors.
* [ST I, q.24, a.1 — Whether the book of life is the same as predestination](st-i-q24-a1.md) - The book of life is in God taken in a metaphorical sense, according to a comparison with human affairs.
* [ST I, q.24, a.2 — Whether the book of life regards only the life of glory of the predestined](st-i-q24-a2.md) - The book of life, as stated above (Article 1), implies a conscription or a knowledge of those chosen to life.
* [ST I, q.24, a.3 — Whether anyone may be blotted out of the book of life](st-i-q24-a3.md) - Some have said that none could be blotted out of the book of life as a matter of fact, but only in the opinion of men.
* [ST I, q.25, a.1 — Whether there is power in God](st-i-q25-a1.md) - Power is twofold—namely, passive, which exists not at all in God;
* [ST I, q.25, a.2 — Whether the power of God is infinite](st-i-q25-a2.md) - As stated above (Article 1), active power exists in God according to the measure in which He is actual.
* [ST I, q.25, a.3 — Whether God is omnipotent](st-i-q25-a3.md) - God can do all things that are possible absolutely, that is, whatever does not imply a contradiction.
* [ST I, q.25, a.4 — Whether God can make the past not to have been](st-i-q25-a4.md) - As was said above (I:7:2), there does not fall under the scope of God's omnipotence anything that implies a contradiction.
* [ST I, q.25, a.5 — Whether God can do what He does not](st-i-q25-a5.md) - In this matter certain persons erred in two ways.
* [ST I, q.25, a.6 — Whether God can do better than what He does](st-i-q25-a6.md) - The goodness of anything is twofold;
* [ST I, q.26, a.1 — Whether beatitude belongs to God](st-i-q26-a1.md) - Beatitude belongs to God in a very special manner.
* [ST I, q.26, a.2 — Whether God is called blessed in respect of His intellect](st-i-q26-a2.md) - Beatitude, as stated above (Article 1), is the perfect good of an intellectual nature.
* [ST I, q.26, a.3 — Whether God is the beatitude of each of the blessed](st-i-q26-a3.md) - The beatitude of an intellectual nature consists in an act of the intellect.
* [ST I, q.26, a.4 — Whether all other beatitude is included in the beatitude of God](st-i-q26-a4.md) - Whatever is desirable in whatsoever beatitude, whether true or false, pre-exists wholly and in a more eminent degree in the divine beatitude.
* [ST I, q.27, a.1 — Whether there is procession in God](st-i-q27-a1.md) - Divine Scripture uses, in relation to God, names which signify procession.
* [ST I, q.27, a.2 — Whether any procession in God can be called generation](st-i-q27-a2.md) - The procession of the Word in God is called generation.
* [ST I, q.27, a.3 — Whether any other procession exists in God besides that of the Word](st-i-q27-a3.md) - There are two processions in God;
* [ST I, q.27, a.4 — Whether the procession of love in God is generation](st-i-q27-a4.md) - The procession of love in God ought not to be called generation.
* [ST I, q.27, a.5 — Whether there are more than two processions in God](st-i-q27-a5.md) - The divine processions can be derived only from the actions which remain within the agent.
* [ST I, q.28, a.1 — Whether there are real relations in God](st-i-q28-a1.md) - relations exist in God really;
* [ST I, q.28, a.2 — Whether relation in God is the same as His essence](st-i-q28-a2.md) - It is reported that Gilbert de la Porree erred on this point, but revoked his error later at the council of Rheims.
* [ST I, q.28, a.3 — Whether the relations in God are really distinguished from each other](st-i-q28-a3.md) - The attributing of anything to another involves the attribution likewise of whatever is contained in it.

* [ST I, q.29, a.1 — The definition of person](st-i-q29-a1.md) - Aquinas accepts Boethius's definition of person as an individual substance of a rational nature.
* [ST I, q.29, a.2 — Whether "person" is the same as hypostasis, subsistence, and essence](st-i-q29-a2.md) - According to the Philosopher (Metaph.
* [ST I, q.29, a.3 — Whether the word "person" should be said of God](st-i-q29-a3.md) - "Person" signifies what is most perfect in all nature —that is, a subsistent individual of a rational nature.
* [ST I, q.29, a.4 — Whether this word "person" signifies relation](st-i-q29-a4.md) - A difficulty arises concerning the meaning of this word "person" in God, from the fact that it is predicated plurally of the Three in contrast to the nature of the names belonging to the essence;
* [ST I, q.30, a.1 — Whether there are several persons in God](st-i-q30-a1.md) - It follows from what precedes that there are several persons in God.
* [ST I, q.30, a.2 — Whether there are more than three persons in God](st-i-q30-a2.md) - As was explained above, there can be only three persons in God.
* [ST I, q.30, a.3 — Whether the numeral terms denote anything real in God](st-i-q30-a3.md) - The Master (Sent.
* [ST I, q.30, a.4 — Whether this term "person" can be common to the three persons](st-i-q30-a4.md) - The very mode of expression itself shows that this term "person" is common to the three when we say "three persons";
* [ST I, q.31, a.1 — Whether there is trinity in God](st-i-q31-a1.md) - The name " Trinity " in God signifies the determinate number of persons.
* [ST I, q.31, a.2 — Whether the Son is other than the Father](st-i-q31-a2.md) - Since as Jerome remarks [In substance, Ep.
* [ST I, q.31, a.3 — Whether the exclusive word "alone" should be added to the essential term in God](st-i-q31-a3.md) - This term "alone" can be taken as a categorematical term, or as a syncategorematical term.
* [ST I, q.31, a.4 — Whether an exclusive diction can be joined to the personal term](st-i-q31-a4.md) - When we say, "The Father alone is God," such a proposition can be taken in several senses.
* [ST I, q.32, a.1 — Whether the trinity of the divine persons can be known by natural reason](st-i-q32-a1.md) - It is impossible to attain to the knowledge of the Trinity by natural reason.
* [ST I, q.32, a.2 — Whether there are notions in God](st-i-q32-a2.md) - Prepositivus, considering the simplicity of the persons, said that in God there were no properties or notions, and wherever there were mentioned, he propounded the abstract for the concrete.
* [ST I, q.32, a.3 — Whether there are five notions](st-i-q32-a3.md) - A notion is the proper idea whereby we know a divine Person.
* [ST I, q.32, a.4 — Whether it is lawful to have various contrary opinions of notions](st-i-q32-a4.md) - Anything is of faith in two ways;
* [ST I, q.33, a.1 — Whether it belongs to the Father to be the principle](st-i-q33-a1.md) - The word "principle" signifies only that whence another proceeds: since anything whence something proceeds in any way we call a principle;

* [ST I, q.33, a.3 — Whether this name "Father" is applied to God, firstly as a personal name](st-i-q33-a3.md) - A name is applied to that wherein is perfectly contained its whole signification, before it is applied to that which only partially contains it;

* [ST I, q.34, a.1 — Whether Word in God is a personal name](st-i-q34-a1.md) - The name of Word in God, if taken in its proper sense, is a personal name, and in no way an essential name.
* [ST I, q.34, a.2 — Whether "Word" is the Son's proper name](st-i-q34-a2.md) - "Word," said of God in its proper sense, is used personally, and is the proper name of the person of the Son.
* [ST I, q.34, a.3 — Whether the name "Word" imports relation to creatures](st-i-q34-a3.md) - Word implies relation to creatures.
* [ST I, q.35, a.1 — Whether image in God is said personally](st-i-q35-a1.md) - Image includes the idea of similitude.
* [ST I, q.35, a.2 — Whether the name of Image is proper to the Son](st-i-q35-a2.md) - The Greek Doctors commonly say that the Holy Ghost is the Image of both the Father and of the Son;

* [ST I, q.36, a.3 — Whether the Holy Ghost proceeds from the Father through the Son](st-i-q36-a3.md) - Whenever one is said to act through another, this preposition "through" points out, in what is covered by it, some cause or principle of that act.
* [ST I, q.36, a.4 — Whether the Father and the Son are one principle of the Holy Ghost](st-i-q36-a4.md) - The Father and the Son are in everything one, wherever there is no distinction between them of opposite relation.
* [ST I, q.37, a.1 — Whether "Love" is the proper name of the Holy Ghost](st-i-q37-a1.md) - The name Love in God can be taken essentially and personally.

* [ST I, q.38, a.1 — Whether "Gift" is a personal name](st-i-q38-a1.md) - The word " gift " imports an aptitude for being given.
* [ST I, q.38, a.2 — Whether "Gift" is the proper name of the Holy Ghost](st-i-q38-a2.md) - Gift, taken personally in God, is the proper name of the Holy Ghost.
* [ST I, q.39, a.1 — Whether in God the essence is the same as the person](st-i-q39-a1.md) - The truth of this question is quite clear if we consider the divine simplicity.
* [ST I, q.39, a.2 — Whether it must be said that the three persons are of one essence](st-i-q39-a2.md) - As above explained (I:13:1 and I:13:2), divine things are named by our intellect, not as they really are in themselves, for in that way it knows them not;
* [ST I, q.39, a.3 — Whether essential names should be predicated in the singular of the three persons](st-i-q39-a3.md) - Some essential names signify the essence after the manner of substantives;
* [ST I, q.39, a.4 — Whether the concrete essential names can stand for the person](st-i-q39-a4.md) - Some have said that this name "God" and the like, properly according to their nature, stand for the essence, but by reason of some notional adjunct are made to stand for the Person.
* [ST I, q.39, a.5 — Whether abstract essential names can stand for the person](st-i-q39-a5.md) - Concerning this, the abbot Joachim erred in asserting that as we can say " God begot God," so we can say "Essence begot essence ": considering that, by reason of the divine simplicity God is…
* [ST I, q.39, a.6 — Whether the persons can be predicated of the essential terms](st-i-q39-a6.md) - As above explained (Article 5), although adjectival terms, whether personal or notional, cannot be predicated of the essence, nevertheless substantive terms can be so predicated, owing to the real…
* [ST I, q.39, a.7 — Whether the essential names should be appropriated to the persons](st-i-q39-a7.md) - For the manifestation of our faith it is fitting that the essential attributes should be appropriated to the persons.
* [ST I, q.39, a.8 — Whether the essential attributes are appropriated to the persons in a fitting manner by the holy doctors](st-i-q39-a8.md) - Our intellect, which is led to the knowledge of God from creatures, must consider God according to the mode derived from creatures.
* [ST I, q.40, a.1 — Whether relation is the same as person](st-i-q40-a1.md) - Different opinions have been held on this point.
* [ST I, q.40, a.2 — Whether the persons are distinguished by the relations](st-i-q40-a2.md) - In whatever multitude of things is to be found something common to all, it is necessary to seek out the principle of distinction.
* [ST I, q.40, a.3 — Whether the hypostases remain if the relations are mentally abstracted from the persons](st-i-q40-a3.md) - Abstraction by the intellect is twofold—when the universal is abstracted from the particular, as animal abstracted from man;
* [ST I, q.40, a.4 — Whether the properties presuppose the notional acts](st-i-q40-a4.md) - According to the opinion that the properties do not distinguish and constitute the hypostases in God, but only manifest them as already distinct and constituted, we must absolutely say that the…
* [ST I, q.41, a.1 — Whether the notional acts are to be attributed to the persons](st-i-q41-a1.md) - In the divine persons distinction is founded on origin.
* [ST I, q.41, a.2 — Whether the notional acts are voluntary](st-i-q41-a2.md) - When anything is said to be, or to be made by the will, this can be understood in two senses.
* [ST I, q.41, a.3 — Whether the notional acts proceed from something](st-i-q41-a3.md) - The Son was not begotten from nothing, but from the Father's substance.
* [ST I, q.41, a.4 — Whether in God there is a power in respect of the notional acts](st-i-q41-a4.md) - As the notional acts exist in God, so must there be also a power in God regarding these acts;
* [ST I, q.41, a.5 — Whether the power of begetting signifies a relation, and not the essence](st-i-q41-a5.md) - Some have said that the power to beget signifies relation in God.
* [ST I, q.41, a.6 — Whether several persons can be the term of one notional act](st-i-q41-a6.md) - As Athanasius says, in God there is only "one Father, one Son, one Holy Ghost." For this four reasons may be given.
* [ST I, q.42, a.1 — Whether there is equality in God](st-i-q42-a1.md) - We must needs admit equality among the divine persons.
* [ST I, q.42, a.2 — Whether the person proceeding is co-eternal with His principle, as the Son with the Father](st-i-q42-a2.md) - We must say that the Son is co-eternal with the Father.
* [ST I, q.42, a.3 — Whether in the divine persons there exists an order of nature](st-i-q42-a3.md) - Order always has reference to some principle.
* [ST I, q.42, a.4 — Whether the Son is equal to the Father in greatness](st-i-q42-a4.md) - The Son is necessarily equal to the Father in greatness.
* [ST I, q.42, a.5 — Whether the Son is in the Father, and conversely](st-i-q42-a5.md) - There are three points of consideration as regards the Father and the Son;
* [ST I, q.42, a.6 — Whether the Son is equal to the Father in power](st-i-q42-a6.md) - The Son is necessarily equal to the Father in power.
* [ST I, q.43, a.1 — Whether a divine person can be properly sent](st-i-q43-a1.md) - the notion of mission includes two things: the habitude of the one sent to the sender;
* [ST I, q.43, a.2 — Whether mission is eternal, or only temporal](st-i-q43-a2.md) - A certain difference is to be observed in all the words that express the origin of the divine persons.
* [ST I, q.43, a.3 — Whether the invisible mission of the divine person is only according to the gift of sanctifying grace](st-i-q43-a3.md) - The divine person is fittingly sent in the sense that He exists newly in any one;
* [ST I, q.43, a.4 — Whether the Father can be fittingly sent](st-i-q43-a4.md) - The very idea of mission means procession from another, and in God it means procession according to origin, as above expounded.

* [ST I, q.43, a.6 — Whether the invisible mission is to all who participate grace](st-i-q43-a6.md) - As above stated (Articles 3, 4 and 5), mission in its very meaning implies that he who is sent either begins to exist where he was not before, as occurs to creatures;
* [ST I, q.43, a.7 — Whether it is fitting for the Holy Ghost to be sent visibly](st-i-q43-a7.md) - God provides for all things according to the nature of each thing.
* [ST I, q.43, a.8 — Whether a divine person is sent only by the person whence He proceeds eternally](st-i-q43-a8.md) - There are different opinions on this point.
* [ST I, q.44, a.1 — Whether it is necessary that every being be created by God](st-i-q44-a1.md) - Whatever is found in something by participation must be caused by that to which it belongs essentially.
* [ST I, q.44, a.2 — Whether primary matter is created by God](st-i-q44-a2.md) - The ancient philosophers gradually, and as it were step by step, advanced to the knowledge of truth.
* [ST I, q.44, a.3 — Whether the exemplar cause is anything besides God](st-i-q44-a3.md) - God is the first exemplar cause of all things.
* [ST I, q.44, a.4 — Whether God is the final cause of all things](st-i-q44-a4.md) - Every agent acts for an end: otherwise one thing would not follow more than another from the action of the agent, unless it were by chance.
* [ST I, q.45, a.1 — Whether to create is to make something from nothing](st-i-q45-a1.md) - As said above (I:44:2), we must consider not only the emanation of a particular being from a particular agent, but also the emanation of all being from the universal cause, which is God;
* [ST I, q.45, a.2 — Whether God can create anything](st-i-q45-a2.md) - Not only is it not impossible that anything should be created by God, but it is necessary to say that all things were created by God, as appears from what has been said (I:44:1.
* [ST I, q.45, a.3 — Whether creation is anything in the creature](st-i-q45-a3.md) - Creation places something in the thing created according to relation only;
* [ST I, q.45, a.4 — Whether to be created belongs to composite and subsisting things](st-i-q45-a4.md) - To be created is, in a manner, to be made, as was shown above (I:44:2 ad 2-3).
* [ST I, q.45, a.5 — Whether it belongs to God alone to create](st-i-q45-a5.md) - It sufficiently appears at the first glance, according to what precedes (Article 1), that to create can be the action of God alone.
* [ST I, q.45, a.6 — Whether to create is proper to any person](st-i-q45-a6.md) - To create is, properly speaking, to cause or produce the being of things.
* [ST I, q.45, a.7 — Whether in creatures is necessarily found a trace of the Trinity](st-i-q45-a7.md) - Every effect in some degree represents its cause, but diversely.
* [ST I, q.45, a.8 — Whether creation is mingled with works of nature and art](st-i-q45-a8.md) - The doubt on this subject arises from the forms which, some said, do not come into existence by the action of nature, but previously exist in matter;
* [ST I, q.46, a.1 — Whether the universe of creatures always existed](st-i-q46-a1.md) - Nothing except God can be eternal.
* [ST I, q.46, a.2 — Whether it is an article of faith that the world began](st-i-q46-a2.md) - That the world had a beginning in time is held by faith alone and cannot be demonstrated, just as its eternity cannot be demonstrated.
* [ST I, q.46, a.3 — Whether the creation of things was in the beginning of time](st-i-q46-a3.md) - The words of Genesis, "In the beginning God created heaven and earth," are expounded in a threefold sense in order to exclude three errors.
* [ST I, q.47, a.1 — Whether the multitude and distinction of things come from God](st-i-q47-a1.md) - The distinction of things has been ascribed to many causes.
* [ST I, q.47, a.2 — Whether the inequality of things is from God](st-i-q47-a2.md) - When Origen wished to refute those who said that the distinction of things arose from the contrary principles of good and evil, he said that in the beginning all things were created equal by God.
* [ST I, q.47, a.3 — Whether there is only one world](st-i-q47-a3.md) - The very order of things created by God shows the unity of the world.
* [ST I, q.48, a.1 — Whether evil is a nature](st-i-q48-a1.md) - Evil is not a being, form or nature, but the absence of a good that ought to be present: a privation.
* [ST I, q.48, a.2 — Whether evil is found in things](st-i-q48-a2.md) - As was said above (I:47:1 and I:47:2), the perfection of the universe requires that there should be inequality in things, so that every grade of goodness may be realized.
* [ST I, q.48, a.3 — Whether evil is in good as in its subject](st-i-q48-a3.md) - As was said above (Article 1), evil imports the absence of good.
* [ST I, q.48, a.4 — Whether evil corrupts the whole good](st-i-q48-a4.md) - Evil cannot wholly consume good.
* [ST I, q.48, a.5 — Whether evil is adequately divided into pain and fault](st-i-q48-a5.md) - Evil, as was said above (Article 3) is the privation of good, which chiefly and of itself consists in perfection and act.
* [ST I, q.48, a.6 — Whether pain has the nature of evil more than fault has](st-i-q48-a6.md) - Fault has the nature of evil more than pain has;
* [ST I, q.49, a.1 — Whether good can be the cause of evil](st-i-q49-a1.md) - It must be said that every evil in some way has a cause.
* [ST I, q.49, a.2 — Whether the supreme good, God, is the cause of evil](st-i-q49-a2.md) - As appears from what was said (Article 1), the evil which consists in the defect of action is always caused by the defect of the agent.
* [ST I, q.49, a.3 — Whether there be one supreme evil which is the cause of every evil](st-i-q49-a3.md) - It appears from what precedes that there is no one first principle of evil, as there is one first principle of good.
* [ST I, q.50, a.1 — Whether an angel is altogether incorporeal](st-i-q50-a1.md) - There must be some incorporeal creatures.
* [ST I, q.50, a.2 — Whether an angel is composed of matter and form](st-i-q50-a2.md) - Some assert that the angels are composed of matter and form;
* [ST I, q.50, a.3 — Whether the angels exist in any great number](st-i-q50-a3.md) - There have been various opinions with regard to the number of the separate substances.
* [ST I, q.50, a.4 — Whether the angels differ in species](st-i-q50-a4.md) - Some have said that all spiritual substances, even souls, are of the one species.
* [ST I, q.50, a.5 — Whether the angels are incorruptible](st-i-q50-a5.md) - It must necessarily be maintained that the angels are incorruptible of their own nature.
* [ST I, q.51, a.1 — Whether the angels have bodies naturally united to them](st-i-q51-a1.md) - The angels have not bodies naturally united to them.
* [ST I, q.51, a.2 — Whether angels assume bodies](st-i-q51-a2.md) - Some have maintained that the angels never assume bodies, but that all that we read in Scripture of apparitions of angels happened in prophetic vision—that is, according to imagination.
* [ST I, q.51, a.3 — Whether the angels exercise functions of life in the bodies assumed](st-i-q51-a3.md) - Some functions of living subjects have something in common with other operations;
* [ST I, q.52, a.1 — Whether an angel is in a place](st-i-q52-a1.md) - It is befitting an angel to be in a place;
* [ST I, q.52, a.2 — Whether an angel can be in several places at once](st-i-q52-a2.md) - An angel's power and nature are finite, whereas the Divine power and essence, which is the universal cause of all things, is infinite: consequently God through His power touches all things, and is…
* [ST I, q.52, a.3 — Whether several angels can be at the same time in the same place](st-i-q52-a3.md) - There are not two angels in the same place.
* [ST I, q.53, a.1 — Whether an angel can be moved locally](st-i-q53-a1.md) - A beatified angel can be moved locally.
* [ST I, q.53, a.2 — Whether an angel passes through intermediate space](st-i-q53-a2.md) - As was observed above in the preceding article, the local motion of an angel can be continuous, and non-continuous.
* [ST I, q.53, a.3 — Whether the movement of an angel is instantaneous](st-i-q53-a3.md) - Some have maintained that the local movement of an angel is instantaneous.
* [ST I, q.54, a.1 — Whether an angel's act of understanding is his substance](st-i-q54-a1.md) - It is impossible for the action of an angel, or of any creature, to be its own substance.
* [ST I, q.54, a.2 — Whether in the angel to understand is to exist](st-i-q54-a2.md) - The action of the angel, as also the action of any creature, is not his existence.

* [ST I, q.110, a.3 — Whether bodies obey the angels as regards local motion](st-i-q110-a3.md) - As Dionysius says (Div.
* [ST I, q.110, a.4 — Whether angels can work miracles](st-i-q110-a4.md) - A miracle properly so called is when something is done outside the order of nature.
* [ST I, q.111, a.1 — Whether an angel can enlighten man](st-i-q111-a1.md) - Since the order of Divine Providence disposes that lower things be subject to the actions of higher, as explained above (I:109:2);
* [ST I, q.111, a.2 — Whether the angels can change the will of man](st-i-q111-a2.md) - The will can be changed in two ways.
* [ST I, q.111, a.3 — Whether an angel can change man's imagination](st-i-q111-a3.md) - Both a good and a bad angel by their own natural power can move the human imagination.
* [ST I, q.111, a.4 — Whether an angel can change the human senses](st-i-q111-a4.md) - The senses may be changed in a twofold manner;
* [ST I, q.112, a.1 — Whether the angels are sent on works of ministry](st-i-q112-a1.md) - From what has been said above (I:108:6), it may be shown that some angels are sent in ministry by God.
* [ST I, q.112, a.2 — Whether all the angels are sent in ministry](st-i-q112-a2.md) - As appears from what has been said above (I:106:3;
* [ST I, q.112, a.3 — Whether all the angels who are sent, assist](st-i-q112-a3.md) - The angels are spoken of as "assisting" and "administering," after the likeness of those who attend upon a king;
* [ST I, q.112, a.4 — Whether all the angels of the second hierarchy are sent](st-i-q112-a4.md) - As above stated (Article 1), to be sent to external ministry properly belongs to an angel according as he acts by Divine command in respect of any corporeal creature;
* [ST I, q.113, a.1 — Whether men are guarded by the angels](st-i-q113-a1.md) - According to the plan of Divine Providence, we find that in all things the movable and variable are moved and regulated by the immovable and invariable;
* [ST I, q.113, a.2 — Whether each man is guarded by an angel](st-i-q113-a2.md) - Each man has an angel guardian appointed to him.
* [ST I, q.113, a.3 — Whether to guard men belongs only to the lowest order of angels](st-i-q113-a3.md) - As above stated (Article 2), man is guarded in two ways;
* [ST I, q.113, a.4 — Whether angels are appointed to the guardianship of all men](st-i-q113-a4.md) - Man while in this state of life, is, as it were, on a road by which he should journey towards heaven.
* [ST I, q.113, a.5 — Whether an angel is appointed to guard a man from his birth](st-i-q113-a5.md) - as Origen observes (Tract.
* [ST I, q.113, a.6 — Whether the angel guardian ever forsakes a man](st-i-q113-a6.md) - As appears above (Article 2), the guardianship of the angels is an effect of Divine providence in regard to man.
* [ST I, q.113, a.7 — Whether angels grieve for the ills of those whom they guard](st-i-q113-a7.md) - Angels do not grieve, either for sins or for the pains inflicted on men.
* [ST I, q.113, a.8 — Whether there can be strife or discord among the angels](st-i-q113-a8.md) - The raising of this question is occasioned by this passage of Daniel.
* [ST I, q.114, a.1 — Whether men are assailed by the demons](st-i-q114-a1.md) - Two things may be considered in the assault of the demons —the assault itself, and the ordering thereof.
* [ST I, q.114, a.2 — Whether to tempt is proper to the devil](st-i-q114-a2.md) - To tempt is, properly speaking, to make trial of something.
* [ST I, q.114, a.3 — Whether all sins are due to the temptation of the devil](st-i-q114-a3.md) - One thing can be the cause of another in two ways;
* [ST I, q.114, a.4 — Whether demons can lead men astray by means of real miracles](st-i-q114-a4.md) - As is clear from what has been said above (I:110:4), if we take a miracle in the strict sense, the demons cannot work miracles, nor can any creature, but God alone: since in the strict sense a…
* [ST I, q.114, a.5 — Whether a demon who is overcome by man, is for this reason hindered from making further assaults](st-i-q114-a5.md) - Some say that when once a demon has been overcome he can no more tempt any man at all, neither to the same nor to any other sin.
* [ST I, q.115, a.1 — Whether a body can be active](st-i-q115-a1.md) - It is apparent to the senses that some bodies are active.
* [ST I, q.115, a.2 — Whether there are any seminal virtues in corporeal matter](st-i-q115-a2.md) - It is customary to name things after what is more perfect, as the Philosopher says (De Anima ii, 4).
* [ST I, q.115, a.3 — Whether the heavenly bodies are the cause of what is produced in bodies here below](st-i-q115-a3.md) - Since every multitude proceeds from unity;
* [ST I, q.115, a.4 — Whether the heavenly bodies are the cause of human actions](st-i-q115-a4.md) - The heavenly bodies can directly and of themselves act on bodies, as stated above (Article 3).
* [ST I, q.115, a.5 — Whether heavenly bodies can act on the demons](st-i-q115-a5.md) - There have been three opinions about the demons.
* [ST I, q.115, a.6 — Whether heavenly bodies impose necessity on things subject to their action](st-i-q115-a6.md) - This question is partly solved by what was said above (Article 4);
* [ST I, q.116, a.1 — Whether there be such a thing as fate](st-i-q116-a1.md) - In this world some things seem to happen by luck or chance.
* [ST I, q.116, a.2 — Whether fate is in created things](st-i-q116-a2.md) - As is clear from what has been stated above (I:22:3;
* [ST I, q.116, a.3 — Whether fate is unchangeable](st-i-q116-a3.md) - The disposition of second causes which we call fate, can be considered in two ways: firstly, in regard to the second causes, which are thus disposed or ordered;
* [ST I, q.116, a.4 — Whether all things are subject to fate](st-i-q116-a4.md) - As stated above (Article 2), fate is the ordering of second causes to effects foreseen by God.
* [ST I, q.117, a.1 — Whether one man can teach another](st-i-q117-a1.md) - On this question there have been various opinions.
* [ST I, q.117, a.2 — Whether man can teach the angels](st-i-q117-a2.md) - Article 2.

* [ST I, q.117, a.4 — Whether the separate human soul can move bodies at least locally](st-i-q117-a4.md) - The separate soul cannot by its natural power move a body.
* [ST I, q.118, a.1 — Whether the sensitive soul is transmitted with the semen](st-i-q118-a1.md) - Some have held that the sensitive souls of animals are created by God (I:65:4.
* [ST I, q.118, a.2 — Whether the intellectual soul is produced from the semen](st-i-q118-a2.md) - It is impossible for an active power existing in matter to extend its action to the production of an immaterial effect.
