* [ST I, q.119, a.1 — Whether some part of the food is changed into true human nature](st-i-q119-a1.md) - According to the Philosopher (Metaph.
* [ST I, q.119, a.2 — Whether the semen is produced from surplus food](st-i-q119-a2.md) - This question depends in some way on what has been stated above (Article 1;

## Prima secundae (I-II)

* [ST I-II, q.1, a.1 — Whether it belongs to man to act for an end](st-i-ii-q1-a1.md) - Properly human acts are those that proceed from deliberate will, and the object of the will is the end and the good.
* [ST I-II, q.1, a.2 — Whether it is proper to the rational nature to act for an end](st-i-ii-q1-a2.md) - Every agent, of necessity, acts for an end.
* [ST I-II, q.1, a.3 — Whether human acts are specified by their end](st-i-ii-q1-a3.md) - Each thing receives its species in respect of an act and not in respect of potentiality;
* [ST I-II, q.1, a.4 — Whether there is a last end of human life](st-i-ii-q1-a4.md) - There cannot be an infinite series of ends, since then nothing would be desired and no action would begin or terminate.
* [ST I-II, q.1, a.5 — Whether one man can have several last ends](st-i-ii-q1-a5.md) - It is impossible for one man's will to be directed at the same time to diverse things, as last ends.
* [ST I-II, q.1, a.6 — Whether man will all, whatsoever he wills, for the last end](st-i-ii-q1-a6.md) - Man must, of necessity, desire all, whatsoever he desires, for the last end.
* [ST I-II, q.1, a.7 — Whether all men have the same last end](st-i-ii-q1-a7.md) - We can speak of the last end in two ways: first, considering only the aspect of last end;
* [ST I-II, q.1, a.8 — Whether other creatures concur in that last end](st-i-ii-q1-a8.md) - As the Philosopher says (Phys.
* [ST I-II, q.2, a.1 — Whether man's happiness consists in wealth](st-i-ii-q2-a1.md) - It is impossible for man's happiness to consist in wealth.
* [ST I-II, q.2, a.2 — Whether man's happiness consists in honors](st-i-ii-q2-a2.md) - It is impossible for happiness to consist in honor.
* [ST I-II, q.2, a.3 — Whether man's happiness consists in fame or glory](st-i-ii-q2-a3.md) - Man's happiness cannot consist in human fame or glory.
* [ST I-II, q.2, a.4 — Whether man's happiness consists in power](st-i-ii-q2-a4.md) - It is impossible for happiness to consist in power;
* [ST I-II, q.2, a.5 — Whether man's happiness consists in any bodily good](st-i-ii-q2-a5.md) - It is impossible for man's happiness to consist in the goods of the body;
* [ST I-II, q.2, a.6 — Whether man's happiness consists in pleasure](st-i-ii-q2-a6.md) - Because bodily delights are more generally known, "the name of pleasure has been appropriated to them" (Ethic.
* [ST I-II, q.2, a.7 — Whether some good of the soul constitutes man's happiness](st-i-ii-q2-a7.md) - As stated above (I-II:1:8), the end is twofold: namely, the thing itself, which we desire to attain, and the use, namely, the attainment or possession of that thing.
* [ST I-II, q.2, a.8 — Whether any created good constitutes man's happiness](st-i-ii-q2-a8.md) - After showing that happiness consists neither in wealth, honor, fame, power, bodily goods nor pleasure, Aquinas concludes that no created good can constitute happiness.
* [ST I-II, q.3, a.1 — Whether happiness is something uncreated](st-i-ii-q3-a1.md) - As stated above (I-II:1:8;
* [ST I-II, q.3, a.2 — Whether happiness is an operation](st-i-ii-q3-a2.md) - In so far as man's happiness is something created, existing in him, we must needs say that it is an operation.
* [ST I-II, q.3, a.3 — Whether happiness is an operation of the sensitive part, or of the intellective part only](st-i-ii-q3-a3.md) - A thing may belong to happiness in three ways: (1) essentially, (2) antecedently, (3) consequently.
* [ST I-II, q.3, a.4 — Whether, if happiness is in the intellective part, it is an operation of the intellect or of the will](st-i-ii-q3-a4.md) - As stated above (I-II:2:6) two things are needed for happiness: one, which is the essence of happiness: the other, that is, as it were, its proper accident, i.e.
* [ST I-II, q.3, a.5 — Whether happiness is an operation of the speculative, or of the practical intellect](st-i-ii-q3-a5.md) - Happiness consists in an operation of the speculative rather than of the practical intellect.
* [ST I-II, q.3, a.6 — Whether happiness consists in the consideration of speculative sciences](st-i-ii-q3-a6.md) - As stated above (Article 2, Reply to Objection 4), man's happiness is twofold, one perfect, the other imperfect.
* [ST I-II, q.3, a.7 — Whether happiness consists in the knowledge of separate substances, namely, angels](st-i-ii-q3-a7.md) - As stated above (Article 6), man's perfect happiness consists not in that which perfects the intellect by some participation, but in that which is so by its essence.
* [ST I-II, q.3, a.8 — Whether man's happiness consists in the vision of the divine essence](st-i-ii-q3-a8.md) - Final and perfect happiness can consist only in the vision of the divine essence.
* [ST I-II, q.4, a.1 — Whether delight is required for happiness](st-i-ii-q4-a1.md) - One thing may be necessary for another in four ways.
* [ST I-II, q.4, a.2 — Whether in happiness vision ranks before delight](st-i-ii-q4-a2.md) - The Philosopher discusses this question (Ethic.
* [ST I-II, q.4, a.3 — Whether comprehension is necessary for happiness](st-i-ii-q4-a3.md) - Since Happiness consists in gaining the last end, those things that are required for Happiness must be gathered from the way in which man is ordered to an end.
* [ST I-II, q.4, a.4 — Whether rectitude of the will is necessary for happiness](st-i-ii-q4-a4.md) - Rectitude of will is necessary for Happiness both antecedently and concomitantly.
* [ST I-II, q.4, a.5 — Whether the body is necessary for man's happiness](st-i-ii-q4-a5.md) - Happiness is twofold;
* [ST I-II, q.4, a.6 — Whether perfection of the body is necessary for happiness](st-i-ii-q4-a6.md) - If we speak of that happiness which man can acquire in this life, it is evident that a well-disposed body is of necessity required for it.
* [ST I-II, q.4, a.7 — Whether any external goods are necessary for happiness](st-i-ii-q4-a7.md) - For imperfect happiness, such as can be had in this life, external goods are necessary, not as belonging to the essence of happiness, but by serving as instruments to happiness, which consists in…
* [ST I-II, q.4, a.8 — Whether the fellowship of friend is necessary for happiness](st-i-ii-q4-a8.md) - If we speak of the happiness of this life, the happy man needs friends, as the Philosopher says (Ethic.
* [ST I-II, q.5, a.1 — Whether man can attain happiness](st-i-ii-q5-a1.md) - Happiness is the attainment of the Perfect Good.
* [ST I-II, q.5, a.2 — Whether one man can be happier than another](st-i-ii-q5-a2.md) - As stated above (I-II:1:8;
* [ST I-II, q.5, a.3 — Whether one can be happy in this life](st-i-ii-q5-a3.md) - A certain participation in happiness can be had in this life, but perfect and true happiness cannot, because this life is subject to unavoidable evils and the good desired cannot be possessed without end.
* [ST I-II, q.5, a.4 — Whether happiness once had can be lost](st-i-ii-q5-a4.md) - If we speak of imperfect happiness, such as can be had in this life, in this sense it can be lost.
* [ST I-II, q.5, a.5 — Whether man can attain happiness by his natural powers](st-i-ii-q5-a5.md) - Imperfect happiness can be acquired by natural powers, in the way virtue is acquired.
* [ST I-II, q.5, a.6 — Whether man attains happiness through the action of some higher creature](st-i-ii-q5-a6.md) - Since every creature is subject to the laws of nature, from the very fact that its power and action are limited: that which surpasses created nature, cannot be done by the power of any creature.
* [ST I-II, q.5, a.7 — Whether any good works are necessary that man may receive happiness from God](st-i-ii-q5-a7.md) - Rectitude of the will, as stated above (I-II:4:4), is necessary for Happiness;
* [ST I-II, q.5, a.8 — Whether every man desires happiness](st-i-ii-q5-a8.md) - Happiness can be considered in two ways.
* [ST I-II, q.6, a.1 — Whether there is anything voluntary in human acts](st-i-ii-q6-a1.md) - The voluntary is what proceeds from an intrinsic principle together with knowledge of the end.
* [ST I-II, q.6, a.2 — Whether there is anything voluntary in irrational animals](st-i-ii-q6-a2.md) - As stated above (Article 1), it is essential to the voluntary act that its principle be within the agent, together with some knowledge of the end.
* [ST I-II, q.6, a.3 — Whether there can be voluntariness without any act](st-i-ii-q6-a3.md) - Voluntary is what proceeds from the will.
* [ST I-II, q.6, a.4 — Whether violence can be done to the will](st-i-ii-q6-a4.md) - The act of the will is twofold: one is its immediate act, as it were, elicited by it, namely, "to wish";
* [ST I-II, q.6, a.5 — Whether violence causes involuntariness](st-i-ii-q6-a5.md) - Violence is directly opposed to the voluntary, as likewise to the natural.
* [ST I-II, q.6, a.6 — Whether fear causes involuntariness simply](st-i-ii-q6-a6.md) - As the Philosopher says (Ethic.
* [ST I-II, q.6, a.7 — Whether concupiscence causes involuntariness](st-i-ii-q6-a7.md) - Concupiscence does not cause involuntariness, but on the contrary makes something to be voluntary.
* [ST I-II, q.6, a.8 — Whether ignorance causes involuntariness](st-i-ii-q6-a8.md) - If ignorance causes involuntariness, it is in so far as it deprives one of knowledge, which is a necessary condition of voluntariness, as was declared above (Article 1).
* [ST I-II, q.7, a.1 — Whether a circumstance is an accident of a human act](st-i-ii-q7-a1.md) - Since, according to the Philosopher (Peri Herm.
* [ST I-II, q.7, a.2 — Whether theologians should take note of the circumstances of human acts](st-i-ii-q7-a2.md) - Circumstances come under the consideration of the theologian, for a threefold reason.
* [ST I-II, q.7, a.3 — Whether the circumstances are properly set forth in the third book of Ethics](st-i-ii-q7-a3.md) - Tully, in his Rhetoric (De Invent.
* [ST I-II, q.7, a.4 — Whether the most important circumstances are "why" and "in what the act consists"](st-i-ii-q7-a4.md) - As stated above (I-II:1:1), acts are properly called human, inasmuch as they are voluntary.
* [ST I-II, q.8, a.1 — Whether the will is of good only](st-i-ii-q8-a1.md) - The will is a rational appetite.
* [ST I-II, q.8, a.2 — Whether volition is of the end only, or also of the means](st-i-ii-q8-a2.md) - The word "voluntas" sometimes designates the power of the will, sometimes its act [See note in Article 1, Reply to Objection 1 above.] Accordingly, if we speak of the will as a power, thus it…
* [ST I-II, q.8, a.3 — Whether the will is moved by the same act to the end and to the means](st-i-ii-q8-a3.md) - Since the end is willed in itself, whereas the means, as such, are only willed for the end, it is evident that the will can be moved to the end, without being moved to the means;
* [ST I-II, q.9, a.1 — Whether the will is moved by the intellect](st-i-ii-q9-a1.md) - A thing requires to be moved by something in so far as it is in potentiality to several things;
* [ST I-II, q.9, a.2 — Whether the will is moved by the sensitive appetite](st-i-ii-q9-a2.md) - As stated above (Article 1), that which is apprehended as good and fitting, moves the will by way of object.
* [ST I-II, q.9, a.3 — Whether the will moves itself](st-i-ii-q9-a3.md) - As stated above (Article 1), it belongs to the will to move the other powers, by reason of the end which is the will's object.
* [ST I-II, q.9, a.4 — Whether the will is moved by an exterior principle](st-i-ii-q9-a4.md) - As far as the will is moved by the object, it is evident that it can be moved by something exterior.
* [ST I-II, q.9, a.5 — Whether the will is moved by a heavenly body](st-i-ii-q9-a5.md) - It is evident that the will can be moved by the heavenly bodies in the same way as it is moved by its object;
* [ST I-II, q.9, a.6 — Whether the will is moved by God alone, as exterior principle](st-i-ii-q9-a6.md) - The movement of the will is from within, as also is the movement of nature.
* [ST I-II, q.10, a.1 — Whether the will is moved to anything naturally](st-i-ii-q10-a1.md) - As Boethius says (De Duabus Nat.) and the Philosopher also (Metaph.
* [ST I-II, q.10, a.2 — Whether the will is moved, of necessity, by its object](st-i-ii-q10-a2.md) - The will is moved in two ways: first, as to the exercise of its act;
* [ST I-II, q.10, a.3 — Whether the will is moved, of necessity, by the lower appetite](st-i-ii-q10-a3.md) - As stated above (I-II:9:2), the passion of the sensitive appetite moves the will, in so far as the will is moved by its object: inasmuch as, to wit, man through being disposed in such and such a…
* [ST I-II, q.10, a.4 — Whether the will is moved of necessity by the exterior mover which is God](st-i-ii-q10-a4.md) - As Dionysius says (Div.
* [ST I-II, q.11, a.1 — Whether to enjoy is an act of the appetitive power](st-i-ii-q11-a1.md) - "Fruitio" [enjoyment] and "fructus" [fruit] seem to refer to the same, one being derived from the other;
* [ST I-II, q.11, a.2 — Whether to enjoy belongs to the rational creature alone, or also to irrational animals](st-i-ii-q11-a2.md) - As was stated above (Article 1) to enjoy is not the act of the power that achieves the end as executor, but of the power that commands the achievement;
* [ST I-II, q.11, a.3 — Whether enjoyment is only of the last end](st-i-ii-q11-a3.md) - As stated above (Article 1) the notion of fruit implies two things: first that it should come last;
* [ST I-II, q.11, a.4 — Whether enjoyment is only of the end possessed](st-i-ii-q11-a4.md) - To enjoy implies a certain relation of the will to the last end, according as the will has something by way of last end.
* [ST I-II, q.12, a.1 — Whether intention is an act of the intellect or of the will](st-i-ii-q12-a1.md) - Intention, as the very word denotes, signifies, "to tend to something." Now both the action of the mover and the movement of thing moved, tend to something.
* [ST I-II, q.12, a.2 — Whether intention is only of the last end](st-i-ii-q12-a2.md) - As stated above (Article 1, Reply to Objection 4), intention regards the end as a terminus of the movement of the will.
* [ST I-II, q.12, a.3 — Whether one can intend two things at the same time](st-i-ii-q12-a3.md) - The expression "two things" may be taken in two ways: they may be ordained to one another or not so ordained.
* [ST I-II, q.12, a.4 — Whether intention of the end is the same act as the volition of the means](st-i-ii-q12-a4.md) - The movement of the will to the end and to the means can be considered in two ways.
* [ST I-II, q.12, a.5 — Whether intention is within the competency of irrational animals](st-i-ii-q12-a5.md) - As stated above (Article 1), to intend is to tend to something;
* [ST I-II, q.13, a.1 — Whether choice is an act of will or of reason](st-i-ii-q13-a1.md) - The word choice implies something belonging to the reason or intellect, and something belonging to the will: for the Philosopher says (Ethic.
* [ST I-II, q.13, a.2 — Whether choice is to be found in irrational animals](st-i-ii-q13-a2.md) - Since choice is the taking of one thing in preference to another it must of necessity be in respect of several things that can be chosen.
* [ST I-II, q.13, a.3 — Whether choice is only of the means, or sometimes also of the end](st-i-ii-q13-a3.md) - As already stated (Article 1, Reply to Objection 2), choice results from the decision or judgment which is, as it were, the conclusion of a practical syllogism.
* [ST I-II, q.13, a.4 — Whether choice is of those things only that are done by us](st-i-ii-q13-a4.md) - Just as intention regards the end, so does choice regard the means.
* [ST I-II, q.13, a.5 — Whether choice is only of possible things](st-i-ii-q13-a5.md) - As stated above (Article 4), our choice is always concerned with our actions.
* [ST I-II, q.13, a.6 — Whether man chooses of necessity or freely](st-i-ii-q13-a6.md) - Man does not choose of necessity.
* [ST I-II, q.14, a.1 — Whether counsel is an inquiry](st-i-ii-q14-a1.md) - Choice, as stated above (I-II:13:1 ad 2;
* [ST I-II, q.14, a.2 — Whether counsel is of the end, or only of the means](st-i-ii-q14-a2.md) - The end is the principle in practical matters: because the reason of the means is to be found in the end.
* [ST I-II, q.14, a.3 — Whether counsel is only of things that we do](st-i-ii-q14-a3.md) - Counsel properly implies a conference held between several;
* [ST I-II, q.14, a.4 — Whether counsel is about all things that we do](st-i-ii-q14-a4.md) - Counsel is a kind of inquiry, as stated above (Article 1).
* [ST I-II, q.14, a.5 — Whether the process of counsel is one of analysis](st-i-ii-q14-a5.md) - In every inquiry one must begin from some principle.
* [ST I-II, q.14, a.6 — Whether the process of counsel is indefinite](st-i-ii-q14-a6.md) - The inquiry of counsel is actually finite on both sides, on that of its principle and on that of its term.
* [ST I-II, q.15, a.1 — Whether consent is an act of the appetitive or of the apprehensive power](st-i-ii-q15-a1.md) - Consent implies application of sense to something.
* [ST I-II, q.15, a.2 — Whether consent is to be found in irrational animals](st-i-ii-q15-a2.md) - Consent, properly speaking, is not in irrational animals.
* [ST I-II, q.15, a.3 — Whether consent is directed to the end or to the means](st-i-ii-q15-a3.md) - Consent is the application of the appetitive movement to something that is already in the power of him who causes the application.
* [ST I-II, q.15, a.4 — Whether consent to the act belongs only to the higher part of the soul](st-i-ii-q15-a4.md) - The final decision belongs to him who holds the highest place, and to whom it belongs to judge of the others;
* [ST I-II, q.16, a.1 — Whether use is an act of the will](st-i-ii-q16-a1.md) - The use of a thing implies the application of that thing to an operation: hence the operation to which we apply a thing is called its use;
* [ST I-II, q.16, a.2 — Whether use is to be found in irrational animals](st-i-ii-q16-a2.md) - as stated above (Article 1), to use is to apply an active principle to action: thus to consent is to apply the appetitive movement to the desire of something, as stated above (Question 15,…
* [ST I-II, q.16, a.3 — Whether use regards also the last end](st-i-ii-q16-a3.md) - Use, as stated above (Article 1), implies the application of one thing to another.
* [ST I-II, q.16, a.4 — Whether use precedes choice](st-i-ii-q16-a4.md) - The will has a twofold relation to the thing willed.
* [ST I-II, q.17, a.1 — Whether command is an act of the reason or of the will](st-i-ii-q17-a1.md) - Command is an act of the reason presupposing, however, an act of the will.
* [ST I-II, q.17, a.2 — Whether command belongs to irrational animals](st-i-ii-q17-a2.md) - To command is nothing else than to direct someone to do something, by a certain motion of intimation.
* [ST I-II, q.17, a.3 — Whether use precedes command](st-i-ii-q17-a3.md) - use of that which is directed to the end, in so far as it is in the reason referring this to the end, precedes choice, as stated above (I-II:16:4).
* [ST I-II, q.17, a.4 — Whether command and the commanded act are one act, or distinct](st-i-ii-q17-a4.md) - Nothing prevents certain things being distinct in one respect, and one in another respect.
* [ST I-II, q.17, a.5 — Whether the act of the will is commanded](st-i-ii-q17-a5.md) - As stated above (Article 1), command is nothing else than the act of the reason directing, with a certain motion, something to act.
* [ST I-II, q.17, a.6 — Whether the act of the reason is commanded](st-i-ii-q17-a6.md) - Since the reason reacts on itself, just as it directs the acts of other powers, so can it direct its own act.
* [ST I-II, q.17, a.7 — Whether the act of the sensitive appetite is commanded](st-i-ii-q17-a7.md) - An act is subject to our command, in so far as it is in our power, as stated above (Article 5).
* [ST I-II, q.17, a.8 — Whether the act of the vegetal soul is commanded](st-i-ii-q17-a8.md) - Some acts proceed from the natural appetite, others from the animal, or from the intellectual appetite: for every agent desires an end in some way.
* [ST I-II, q.17, a.9 — Whether the acts of the external members are commanded](st-i-ii-q17-a9.md) - The members of the body are organs of the soul's powers.
* [ST I-II, q.18, a.1 — Whether every human action is good, or are there evil actions](st-i-ii-q18-a1.md) - We must speak of good and evil in actions as of good and evil in things: because such as everything is, such is the act that it produces.
* [ST I-II, q.18, a.2 — Whether the good or evil of a man's action is derived from its object](st-i-ii-q18-a2.md) - as stated above (Article 1) the good or evil of an action, as of other things, depends on its fulness of being or its lack of that fulness.
* [ST I-II, q.18, a.3 — Whether man's action is good or evil from a circumstance](st-i-ii-q18-a3.md) - In natural things, it is to be noted that the whole fulness of perfection due to a thing, is not from the mere substantial form, that gives it its species;
* [ST I-II, q.18, a.4 — Whether the goodness or malice of a human action depends on the end](st-i-ii-q18-a4.md) - A human act has a fourfold goodness: generic, as an action;
* [ST I-II, q.18, a.5 — Whether a human action is good or evil in its species](st-i-ii-q18-a5.md) - Every action derives its species from its object, as stated above (Article 2).
* [ST I-II, q.18, a.6 — Whether an action has the species of good or evil from its end](st-i-ii-q18-a6.md) - Certain actions are called human, inasmuch as they are voluntary, as stated above (I-II:1:1).
* [ST I-II, q.18, a.7 — Whether the species derived from the end is contained under the species derived from the object, as under its genus, or conversely](st-i-ii-q18-a7.md) - The object of the external act can stand in a twofold relation to the end of the will: first, as being of itself ordained thereto;
* [ST I-II, q.18, a.8 — Whether any action is indifferent in its species](st-i-ii-q18-a8.md) - As stated above (Articles 2 and 5), every action takes its species from its object;
* [ST I-II, q.18, a.9 — Whether an individual action can be indifferent](st-i-ii-q18-a9.md) - It sometimes happens that an action is indifferent in its species, but considered in the individual it is good or evil.
* [ST I-II, q.18, a.10 — Whether a circumstance places a moral action in the species of good or evil](st-i-ii-q18-a10.md) - Just as the species of natural things are constituted by their natural forms, so the species of moral actions are constituted by forms as conceived by the reason, as is evident from what was said…
* [ST I-II, q.18, a.11 — Whether every circumstance that makes an action better or worse, places a moral action in a species of good or evil](st-i-ii-q18-a11.md) - As stated above (Article 10), a circumstance gives the species of good or evil to a moral action, in so far as it regards a special order of reason.
* [ST I-II, q.19, a.1 — Whether the goodness of the will depends on the object](st-i-ii-q19-a1.md) - Good and evil are essential differences of the act of the will.
* [ST I-II, q.19, a.2 — Whether the goodness of the will depends on the object alone](st-i-ii-q19-a2.md) - In every genus, the more a thing is first, the more simple it is, and the fewer the principles of which it consists: thus primary bodies are simple.
* [ST I-II, q.19, a.3 — Whether the goodness of the will depends on reason](st-i-ii-q19-a3.md) - As stated above (Articles 1 and 2), the goodness of the will depends properly on the object.
* [ST I-II, q.19, a.4 — Whether the goodness of the will depends on the eternal law](st-i-ii-q19-a4.md) - Wherever a number of causes are subordinate to one another, the effect depends more on the first than on the second cause: since the second cause acts only in virtue of the first.
* [ST I-II, q.19, a.5 — Whether the will is evil when it is at variance with erring reason](st-i-ii-q19-a5.md) - Every conscience, whether right or erring, binds: whoever acts against the judgment of his reason, even if mistaken, has an evil will, because he wills what he apprehends as evil.
* [ST I-II, q.19, a.6 — Whether the will is good when it abides by erring reason](st-i-ii-q19-a6.md) - Following an erring conscience excuses from sin only if the error is involuntary and not culpable.
* [ST I-II, q.19, a.7 — Whether the goodness of the will, as regards the means, depends on the intention of the end](st-i-ii-q19-a7.md) - The intention may stand in a twofold relation to the act of the will;
* [ST I-II, q.19, a.8 — Whether the degree of goodness or malice in the will depends on the degree of good or evil in the intention](st-i-ii-q19-a8.md) - In regard to both the act, and the intention of the end, we may consider a twofold quantity: one, on the part of the object, by reason of a man willing or doing a good that is greater;
* [ST I-II, q.19, a.9 — Whether the goodness of the will depends on its conformity to the Divine will](st-i-ii-q19-a9.md) - As stated above (Article 7), the goodness of the will depends on the intention of the end.
* [ST I-II, q.19, a.10 — Whether it is necessary for the human will, in order to be good, to be conformed to the Divine will, as regards the thing willed](st-i-ii-q19-a10.md) - As is evident from what has been said above (Articles 3 and 5), the will tends to its object, according as it is proposed by the reason.
* [ST I-II, q.20, a.1 — Whether goodness or malice is first in the action of the will, or in the external action](st-i-ii-q20-a1.md) - External actions may be said to be good or bad in two ways.
* [ST I-II, q.20, a.2 — Whether the whole goodness and malice of the external action depends on the goodness of the will](st-i-ii-q20-a2.md) - As stated above (Article 1), we may consider a twofold goodness or malice in the external action: one in respect of due matter and circumstances;
* [ST I-II, q.20, a.3 — Whether the goodness and malice of the external action are the same as those of the interior act](st-i-ii-q20-a3.md) - As stated above (I-II:17:4), the interior act of the will, and the external action, considered morally, are one act.
* [ST I-II, q.20, a.4 — Whether the external action adds any goodness or malice to that of the interior act](st-i-ii-q20-a4.md) - If we speak of the goodness which the external action derives from the will tending to the end, then the external action adds nothing to this goodness, unless it happens that the will in itself is…
* [ST I-II, q.20, a.5 — Whether the consequences of the external action increase its goodness or malice](st-i-ii-q20-a5.md) - The consequences of an action are either foreseen or not.
* [ST I-II, q.20, a.6 — Whether one and the same external action can be both good and evil](st-i-ii-q20-a6.md) - Nothing hinders a thing from being one, in so far as it is in one genus, and manifold, in so far as it is referred to another genus.
* [ST I-II, q.21, a.1 — Whether a human action is right or sinful, in so far as it is good or evil](st-i-ii-q21-a1.md) - Evil is more comprehensive than sin, as also is good than right.
* [ST I-II, q.21, a.2 — Whether a human action deserves praise or blame, by reason of its being good or evil](st-i-ii-q21-a2.md) - Just as evil is more comprehensive than sin, so is sin more comprehensive than blame.
* [ST I-II, q.21, a.3 — Whether a human action is meritorious or demeritorious in so far as it is good or evil](st-i-ii-q21-a3.md) - We speak of merit and demerit, in relation to retribution, rendered according to justice.
* [ST I-II, q.21, a.4 — Whether a human action is meritorious or demeritorious before God, according as it is good or evil](st-i-ii-q21-a4.md) - A human action, as stated above (Article 3), acquires merit or demerit, through being ordained to someone else, either by reason of himself, or by reason of the community: and in each way, our…
* [ST I-II, q.22, a.1 — Whether any passion is in the soul](st-i-ii-q22-a1.md) - The word "passive" is used in three ways.
* [ST I-II, q.22, a.2 — Whether passion is in the appetitive rather than in the apprehensive part](st-i-ii-q22-a2.md) - As we have already stated (Article 1) the word "passion" implies that the patient is drawn to that which belongs to the agent.
* [ST I-II, q.22, a.3 — Whether passion is in the sensitive appetite rather than in the intellectual appetite, which is called the will](st-i-ii-q22-a3.md) - As stated above (Article 1) passion is properly to be found where there is corporeal transmutation.
* [ST I-II, q.23, a.1 — Whether the passions of the concupiscible part are different from those of the irascible part](st-i-ii-q23-a1.md) - The passions of the irascible part differ in species from those of the concupiscible faculty.
* [ST I-II, q.23, a.2 — Whether the contrariety of the irascible passions is based on the contrariety of good and evil](st-i-ii-q23-a2.md) - Passion is a kind of movement, as stated in Phys.
* [ST I-II, q.23, a.3 — Whether any passion of the soul has no contrariety](st-i-ii-q23-a3.md) - The passion of anger is peculiar in this, that it cannot have a contrary, either according to approach and withdrawal, or according to the contrariety of good and evil.
* [ST I-II, q.23, a.4 — Whether in the same power, there are any passions, specifically different, but not contrary to one another](st-i-ii-q23-a4.md) - Passions differ in accordance with their active causes, which, in the case of the passions of the soul, are their objects.
* [ST I-II, q.24, a.1 — Whether moral good and evil can be found in the passions of the soul](st-i-ii-q24-a1.md) - We may consider the passions of the soul in two ways: first, in themselves;
* [ST I-II, q.24, a.2 — Whether every passion of the soul is evil morally](st-i-ii-q24-a2.md) - On this question the opinion of the Stoics differed from that of the Peripatetics: for the Stoics held that all passions are evil, while the Peripatetics maintained that moderate passions are good.
* [ST I-II, q.24, a.3 — Whether passion increases or decreases the goodness or malice of an act](st-i-ii-q24-a3.md) - As the Stoics held that every passion of the soul is evil, they consequently held that every passion of the soul lessens the goodness of an act;
* [ST I-II, q.24, a.4 — Whether any passion is good or evil in its species](st-i-ii-q24-a4.md) - We ought, seemingly, to apply to passions what has been said in regard to acts (I-II:18:5 and I-II:18:6;
* [ST I-II, q.25, a.1 — Whether the irascible passions precede the concupiscible passions, or vice versa](st-i-ii-q25-a1.md) - In the concupiscible passions there is more diversity than in the passions of the irascible faculty.
* [ST I-II, q.25, a.2 — Whether love is the first of the concupiscible passions](st-i-ii-q25-a2.md) - Good and evil are the object of the concupiscible faculty.
* [ST I-II, q.25, a.3 — Whether hope is the first of the irascible passions](st-i-ii-q25-a3.md) - As stated above (Article 1) all irascible passions imply movement towards something.
* [ST I-II, q.25, a.4 — Whether these are the four principal passions: joy, sadness, hope and fear](st-i-ii-q25-a4.md) - These four are commonly called the principal passions.
* [ST I-II, q.26, a.1 — Whether love is in the concupiscible power](st-i-ii-q26-a1.md) - Love is something pertaining to the appetite;
* [ST I-II, q.26, a.2 — Whether love is a passion](st-i-ii-q26-a2.md) - Passion is the effect of the agent on the patient.
* [ST I-II, q.26, a.3 — Whether love is the same as dilection](st-i-ii-q26-a3.md) - We find four words referring in a way, to the same thing: viz.
* [ST I-II, q.26, a.4 — Whether love is properly divided into love of friendship and love of concupiscence](st-i-ii-q26-a4.md) - To love is to will good to someone.
* [ST I-II, q.27, a.1 — Whether good is the only cause of love](st-i-ii-q27-a1.md) - As stated above (I-II:26:1), Love belongs to the appetitive power which is a passive faculty.
* [ST I-II, q.27, a.2 — Whether knowledge is a cause of love](st-i-ii-q27-a2.md) - As stated above (Article 1), good is the cause of love, as being its object.
* [ST I-II, q.27, a.3 — Whether likeness is a cause of love](st-i-ii-q27-a3.md) - Likeness, properly speaking, is a cause of love.
* [ST I-II, q.27, a.4 — Whether any other passion of the soul is a cause of love](st-i-ii-q27-a4.md) - There is no other passion of the soul that does not presuppose love of some kind.
* [ST I-II, q.28, a.1 — Whether union is an effect of love](st-i-ii-q28-a1.md) - The union of lover and beloved is twofold.
* [ST I-II, q.28, a.2 — Whether mutual indwelling is an effect of love](st-i-ii-q28-a2.md) - This effect of mutual indwelling may be understood as referring both to the apprehensive and to the appetitive power.
* [ST I-II, q.28, a.3 — Whether ecstasy is an effect of love](st-i-ii-q28-a3.md) - To suffer ecstasy means to be placed outside oneself.
* [ST I-II, q.28, a.4 — Whether zeal is an effect of love](st-i-ii-q28-a4.md) - Zeal, whatever way we take it, arises from the intensity of love.
* [ST I-II, q.28, a.5 — Whether love is a passion that wounds the lover](st-i-ii-q28-a5.md) - As stated above (I-II:26:1 and I-II:26:2;
* [ST I-II, q.28, a.6 — Whether love is cause of all that the lover does](st-i-ii-q28-a6.md) - Every agent acts for an end, as stated above (I-II:1:2).
* [ST I-II, q.29, a.1 — Whether evil is the cause and object of hatred](st-i-ii-q29-a1.md) - Since the natural appetite is the result of apprehension (though this apprehension is not in the same subject as the natural appetite), it seems that what applies to the inclination of the natural…
* [ST I-II, q.29, a.2 — Whether love is a cause of hatred](st-i-ii-q29-a2.md) - As stated above (Article 1), love consists in a certain agreement of the lover with the object loved, while hatred consists in a certain disagreement or dissonance.
* [ST I-II, q.29, a.3 — Whether hatred is stronger than love](st-i-ii-q29-a3.md) - It is impossible for an effect to be stronger than its cause.
* [ST I-II, q.29, a.4 — Whether a man can hate himself](st-i-ii-q29-a4.md) - Properly speaking, it is impossible for a man to hate himself.
* [ST I-II, q.29, a.5 — Whether a man can hate the truth](st-i-ii-q29-a5.md) - Good, true and being are the same in reality, but differ as considered by reason.
* [ST I-II, q.29, a.6 — Whether anything can be an object of universal hatred](st-i-ii-q29-a6.md) - There are two ways of speaking of the universal: first, as considered under the aspect of universality;
* [ST I-II, q.30, a.1 — Whether concupiscence is in the sensitive appetite only](st-i-ii-q30-a1.md) - As the Philosopher says (Rhet.
* [ST I-II, q.30, a.2 — Whether concupiscence is a specific passion](st-i-ii-q30-a2.md) - As stated above (Article 1;
* [ST I-II, q.30, a.3 — Whether some concupiscences are natural, and some not natural](st-i-ii-q30-a3.md) - As stated above (Article 1), concupiscence is the craving for pleasurable good.
* [ST I-II, q.30, a.4 — Whether concupiscence is infinite](st-i-ii-q30-a4.md) - As stated above (Article 3), concupiscence is twofold;
* [ST I-II, q.31, a.1 — Whether delight is a passion](st-i-ii-q31-a1.md) - The movements of the sensitive appetite, are properly called passions, as stated above (I-II:22:3).
* [ST I-II, q.31, a.2 — Whether delight is in time](st-i-ii-q31-a2.md) - A thing may be in time in two ways: first, by itself;
* [ST I-II, q.31, a.3 — Whether delight differs from joy](st-i-ii-q31-a3.md) - Joy, as Avicenna states (De Anima iv), is a kind of delight.
* [ST I-II, q.31, a.4 — Whether delight is in the intellectual appetite](st-i-ii-q31-a4.md) - As stated above (Article 3), a certain delight arises from the apprehension of the reason.
* [ST I-II, q.31, a.5 — Whether bodily and sensible pleasures are greater than spiritual and intellectual pleasures](st-i-ii-q31-a5.md) - As stated above (Article 1), pleasure arises from union with a suitable object perceived or known.
* [ST I-II, q.31, a.6 — Whether the pleasures of touch are greater than the pleasures afforded by the other senses](st-i-ii-q31-a6.md) - As stated above (I-II:25:2 ad 1;
* [ST I-II, q.31, a.7 — Whether any pleasure is not natural](st-i-ii-q31-a7.md) - We speak of that as being natural, which is in accord with nature, as stated in Phys.
* [ST I-II, q.31, a.8 — Whether one pleasure can be contrary to another](st-i-ii-q31-a8.md) - Pleasure, in the emotions of the soul, is likened to repose in natural bodies, as stated above (I-II:23:4).
* [ST I-II, q.32, a.1 — Whether operation is the proper cause of pleasure](st-i-ii-q32-a1.md) - As stated above (I-II:31:1), two things are requisite for pleasure: namely, the attainment of the suitable good, and knowledge of this attainment.
* [ST I-II, q.32, a.2 — Whether movement is a cause of pleasure](st-i-ii-q32-a2.md) - Three things are requisite for pleasure;
* [ST I-II, q.32, a.3 — Whether hope and memory causes pleasure](st-i-ii-q32-a3.md) - Pleasure is caused by the presence of suitable good, in so far as it is felt, or perceived in any way.
* [ST I-II, q.32, a.4 — Whether sadness causes pleasure](st-i-ii-q32-a4.md) - Sadness may be considered in two ways: as existing actually, and as existing in the memory: and in both ways sadness can cause pleasure.
* [ST I-II, q.32, a.5 — Whether the actions of others are a cause of pleasure to us](st-i-ii-q32-a5.md) - As stated above (Article 1;
* [ST I-II, q.32, a.6 — Whether doing good to another is a cause of pleasure](st-i-ii-q32-a6.md) - Doing good to another may give pleasure in three ways.
* [ST I-II, q.32, a.7 — Whether likeness is a cause of pleasure](st-i-ii-q32-a7.md) - Likeness is a kind of unity;
* [ST I-II, q.32, a.8 — Whether wonder is a cause of pleasure](st-i-ii-q32-a8.md) - It is pleasant to get what one desires, as stated above (I-II:23:4): and therefore the greater the desire for the thing loved, the greater the pleasure when it is attained: indeed the very…
* [ST I-II, q.33, a.1 — Whether expansion is an effect of pleasure](st-i-ii-q33-a1.md) - Breadth [latitudo] is a dimension of bodily magnitude: hence it is not applied to the emotions of the soul, save metaphorically.
* [ST I-II, q.33, a.2 — Whether pleasure causes thirst or desire for itself](st-i-ii-q33-a2.md) - Pleasure can be considered in two ways;
* [ST I-II, q.33, a.3 — Whether pleasure hinders the use of reason](st-i-ii-q33-a3.md) - As is stated in Ethic.
* [ST I-II, q.33, a.4 — Whether pleasure perfects operation](st-i-ii-q33-a4.md) - Pleasure perfects operation in two ways.
* [ST I-II, q.34, a.1 — Whether every pleasure is evil](st-i-ii-q34-a1.md) - As stated in Ethic.
* [ST I-II, q.34, a.2 — Whether every pleasure is good](st-i-ii-q34-a2.md) - While some of the Stoics maintained that all pleasures are evil, the Epicureans held that pleasure is good in itself, and that consequently all pleasures are good.
* [ST I-II, q.34, a.3 — Whether any pleasure is the greatest good](st-i-ii-q34-a3.md) - Plato held neither with the Stoics, who asserted that all pleasures are evil, nor with the Epicureans, who maintained that all pleasures are good;
* [ST I-II, q.34, a.4 — Whether pleasure is the measure or rule by which to judge of moral good or evil](st-i-ii-q34-a4.md) - Moral goodness or malice depends chiefly on the will, as stated above (I-II:20:1);
* [ST I-II, q.35, a.1 — Whether pain is a passion of the soul](st-i-ii-q35-a1.md) - Just as two things are requisite for pleasure;
* [ST I-II, q.35, a.2 — Whether sorrow is the same as pain](st-i-ii-q35-a2.md) - Pleasure and pain can arise from a twofold apprehension, namely, from the apprehension of an exterior sense;
* [ST I-II, q.35, a.3 — Whether sorrow or pain is contrary to pleasure](st-i-ii-q35-a3.md) - As the Philosopher says (Metaph.
* [ST I-II, q.35, a.4 — Whether all sorrow is contrary to all pleasure](st-i-ii-q35-a4.md) - As stated in Metaph.
* [ST I-II, q.35, a.5 — Whether there is any sorrow contrary to the pleasure of contemplation](st-i-ii-q35-a5.md) - The pleasure of contemplation can be understood in two ways.
* [ST I-II, q.35, a.6 — Whether sorrow is to be shunned more than pleasure is to be sought](st-i-ii-q35-a6.md) - The desire for pleasure is of itself more eager than the shunning of sorrow.
* [ST I-II, q.35, a.7 — Whether outward pain is greater than interior sorrow](st-i-ii-q35-a7.md) - Interior and exterior pain agree in one point and differ in two.
* [ST I-II, q.35, a.8 — Whether there are only four species of sorrow](st-i-ii-q35-a8.md) - It belongs to the notion of a species that it is something added to the genus.
* [ST I-II, q.36, a.1 — Whether sorrow is caused by the loss of good or by the presence of evil](st-i-ii-q36-a1.md) - If privations, as considered by the mind, were what they are in reality, this question would seem to be of no importance.
* [ST I-II, q.36, a.2 — Whether desire is a cause of sorrow](st-i-ii-q36-a2.md) - Sorrow is a movement of the animal appetite.
* [ST I-II, q.36, a.3 — Whether the craving for unity is a cause of sorrow](st-i-ii-q36-a3.md) - Forasmuch as the desire or craving for good is reckoned as a cause of sorrow, so must a craving for unity, and love, be accounted as causing sorrow.
* [ST I-II, q.36, a.4 — Whether an irresistible power is a cause of sorrow](st-i-ii-q36-a4.md) - As stated above (Article 1), a present evil, is cause of sorrow or pain, by way of object.
* [ST I-II, q.37, a.1 — Whether pain deprives one of the power to learn](st-i-ii-q37-a1.md) - Since all the powers of the soul are rooted in the one essence of the soul, it must needs happen, when the intention of the soul is strongly drawn towards the action of one power, that it is…
* [ST I-II, q.37, a.2 — Whether the effect of sorrow or pain is to burden the soul](st-i-ii-q37-a2.md) - The effects of the soul's passions are sometimes named metaphorically, from a likeness to sensible bodies: for the reason that the movements of the animal appetite are like the inclinations of the…
* [ST I-II, q.37, a.3 — Whether sorrow or pain weakens all activity](st-i-ii-q37-a3.md) - As stated above (Article 2), sorrow at times does not depress or consume the soul, so as to shut out all movement, internal or external;
* [ST I-II, q.37, a.4 — Whether sorrow is more harmful to the body than the other passions of the soul](st-i-ii-q37-a4.md) - Of all the soul's passions, sorrow is most harmful to the body.
* [ST I-II, q.38, a.1 — Whether pain or sorrow is assuaged by every pleasure](st-i-ii-q38-a1.md) - As is evident from what has been said above (I-II:23:4), pleasure is a kind of repose of the appetite in a suitable good;
* [ST I-II, q.38, a.2 — Whether pain or sorrow is assuaged by tears](st-i-ii-q38-a2.md) - Tears and groans naturally assuage sorrow: and this for two reasons.
* [ST I-II, q.38, a.3 — Whether pain or sorrow are assuaged by the sympathy of friends](st-i-ii-q38-a3.md) - When one is in pain, it is natural that the sympathy of a friend should afford consolation: whereof the Philosopher indicates a twofold reason (Ethic.
* [ST I-II, q.38, a.4 — Whether pain and sorrow are assuaged by the contemplation of truth](st-i-ii-q38-a4.md) - As stated above (I-II:3:5), the greatest of all pleasures consists in the contemplation of truth.
* [ST I-II, q.38, a.5 — Whether pain and sorrow are assuaged by sleep and baths](st-i-ii-q38-a5.md) - As stated above (I-II:37:4), sorrow, by reason of its specific nature, is repugnant to the vital movement of the body;
* [ST I-II, q.39, a.1 — Whether all sorrow is evil](st-i-ii-q39-a1.md) - A thing may be good or evil in two ways: first considered simply and in itself;
* [ST I-II, q.39, a.2 — Whether sorrow can be a virtuous good](st-i-ii-q39-a2.md) - In so far as sorrow is good, it can be a virtuous good.
* [ST I-II, q.39, a.3 — Whether sorrow can be a useful good](st-i-ii-q39-a3.md) - A twofold movement of the appetite ensues from a present evil.
* [ST I-II, q.39, a.4 — Whether bodily pain is the greatest evil](st-i-ii-q39-a4.md) - It is impossible for any sorrow or pain to be man's greatest evil.
* [ST I-II, q.40, a.1 — Whether hope is the same as desire of cupidity](st-i-ii-q40-a1.md) - The species of a passion is taken from the object.
* [ST I-II, q.40, a.2 — Whether hope is in the apprehensive or in the appetitive power](st-i-ii-q40-a2.md) - Since hope denotes a certain stretching out of the appetite towards good, it evidently belongs to the appetitive power;
* [ST I-II, q.40, a.3 — Whether hope is in dumb animals](st-i-ii-q40-a3.md) - The internal passions of animals can be gathered from their outward movements: from which it is clear that hope is in dumb animals.
* [ST I-II, q.40, a.4 — Whether despair is contrary to hope](st-i-ii-q40-a4.md) - As stated above (I-II:23:2), there is a twofold contrariety of movements.
* [ST I-II, q.40, a.5 — Whether experience is a cause of hope](st-i-ii-q40-a5.md) - As stated above (Article 1), the object of hope is a future good, difficult but possible to obtain.
* [ST I-II, q.40, a.6 — Whether hope abounds in young men and drunkards](st-i-ii-q40-a6.md) - Youth is a cause of hope for three reasons, as the Philosopher states in Rhet.
* [ST I-II, q.40, a.7 — Whether hope is a cause of love](st-i-ii-q40-a7.md) - Hope can regard two things.
* [ST I-II, q.40, a.8 — Whether hope is a help or a hindrance to action](st-i-ii-q40-a8.md) - Hope of its very nature is a help to action by making it more intense: and this for two reasons.
* [ST I-II, q.41, a.1 — Whether fear is a passion of the soul](st-i-ii-q41-a1.md) - Among the other passions of the soul, after sorrow, fear chiefly has the character of passion.
* [ST I-II, q.41, a.2 — Whether fear is a special passion](st-i-ii-q41-a2.md) - The passions of the soul derive their species from their objects: hence that is a special passion, which has a special object.
* [ST I-II, q.41, a.3 — Whether there is a natural fear](st-i-ii-q41-a3.md) - A movement is said to be natural, because nature inclines thereto.
* [ST I-II, q.41, a.4 — Whether the species of fear is suitably assigned](st-i-ii-q41-a4.md) - As stated above (Article 2), fear regards a future evil which surpasses the power of him that fears, so that it is irresistible.
* [ST I-II, q.42, a.1 — Whether the object of fear is good or evil](st-i-ii-q42-a1.md) - Fear is a movement of the appetitive power.
* [ST I-II, q.42, a.2 — Whether evil of nature is an object of fear](st-i-ii-q42-a2.md) - As the Philosopher says (Rhet.
* [ST I-II, q.42, a.3 — Whether the evil of sin is an object of fear](st-i-ii-q42-a3.md) - As stated above (I-II:40:1;
* [ST I-II, q.42, a.4 — Whether fear itself can be feared](st-i-ii-q42-a4.md) - As stated above (Article 3), nothing can be an object of fear, save what is due to an extrinsic cause;
* [ST I-II, q.42, a.5 — Whether sudden things are especially feared](st-i-ii-q42-a5.md) - As stated above (Article 3;
* [ST I-II, q.42, a.6 — Whether those things are more feared, for which there is no remedy](st-i-ii-q42-a6.md) - The object of fear is evil: consequently whatever tends to increase evil, conduces to the increase of fear.
* [ST I-II, q.43, a.1 — Whether love is the cause of fear](st-i-ii-q43-a1.md) - The objects of the soul's passions stand in relation thereto as the forms to things natural or artificial: because the passions of the soul take their species from their objects, as the aforesaid…
* [ST I-II, q.43, a.2 — Whether defect is the cause of fear](st-i-ii-q43-a2.md) - As stated above (Article 1), fear may be set down to a twofold cause: one is by way of a material disposition, on the part of him that fears;
* [ST I-II, q.44, a.1 — Whether fear causes contraction](st-i-ii-q44-a1.md) - As stated above (I-II:28:5), in the passions of the soul, the formal element is the movement of the appetitive power, while the bodily transmutation is the material element.
* [ST I-II, q.44, a.2 — Whether fear makes one suitable for counsel](st-i-ii-q44-a2.md) - A man of counsel may be taken in two ways.
* [ST I-II, q.44, a.3 — Whether fear makes one tremble](st-i-ii-q44-a3.md) - As stated above (Article 1), in fear there takes place a certain contraction from the outward to the inner parts of the body, the result being that the outer parts become cold;
* [ST I-II, q.44, a.4 — Whether fear hinders action](st-i-ii-q44-a4.md) - Man's exterior actions are caused by the soul as first mover, but by the bodily members as instruments.
* [ST I-II, q.45, a.1 — Whether daring is contrary to fear](st-i-ii-q45-a1.md) - It is of the essence of contraries to be "farthest removed from one another," as stated in Metaph.
* [ST I-II, q.45, a.2 — Whether daring ensues from hope](st-i-ii-q45-a2.md) - As we have often stated (I-II:22:2;
* [ST I-II, q.45, a.3 — Whether some defect is a cause of daring](st-i-ii-q45-a3.md) - As stated above (Articles 1 and 2) daring results from hope and is contrary to fear: wherefore whatever is naturally apt to cause hope or banish fear, is a cause of daring.
* [ST I-II, q.45, a.4 — Whether the brave are more eager at first than in the midst of danger](st-i-ii-q45-a4.md) - Daring, being a movement of the sensitive appetite, follows an apprehension of the sensitive faculty.
* [ST I-II, q.46, a.1 — Whether anger is a special passion](st-i-ii-q46-a1.md) - A thing is said to be general in two ways.

* [ST I-II, q.46, a.3 — Whether anger is in the concupiscible faculty](st-i-ii-q46-a3.md) - As stated above (I-II:23:1), the passions of the irascible part differ from the passions of the concupiscible faculty, in that the objects of the concupiscible passions are good and evil…

* [ST I-II, q.46, a.5 — Whether anger is more natural than desire](st-i-ii-q46-a5.md) - By " natural " we mean that which is caused by nature, as stated in Phys.

## Otras obras

* [De ente et essentia, c.4 — On being and essence, chapter 4](dee-c4.md) - Aquinas argues that we can understand what something is without knowing whether it exists, so in creatures existence (esse) is other than essence.
* [De regno, I, c.1 — On kingship, book I, chapter 1](dr-i-c1.md) - Man is by nature a social and political animal, living in a multitude, because he cannot provide sufficiently for his life alone and has language to communicate with others.
* [De veritate, q.1, a.1 — What is truth](dv-q1-a1.md) - Aquinas derives the transcendentals (thing, one, something, good, true) from being.
* [SCG I, c.7 — That the truth of reason is not opposed to the truth of the Christian faith](scg-i-c7.md) - Aquinas holds that what reason naturally knows cannot be contrary to the truth of faith, since both come from God, the author of our nature and of revelation.
* [SCG III, c.37 — That the ultimate happiness of man consists in the contemplation of God](scg-iii-c37.md) - Man's ultimate happiness does not consist in moral actions, which are ordered to something further, but in the contemplation of truth, and specifically in the contemplation of God, to which all human operations seem…
